Mark was, however, aware that a person of Christopher’s way of thinking might well feel inhibited from taking on a cure of souls if, in spite of the fact that he had abstained from seducing a young woman, he nevertheless privately desired to enter into illicit relations with her, and that that was sufficient to justify him in saying that an insuperable obstacle existed. He did not know that he himself agreed, but it was not his business to interfere between any man and his conscience in a matter of the Church. He was himself no very good Christian, at any rate as regards the relationships of men and women. Nevertheless, the Church of England was the Church of England. No doubt, had Christopher been a Papist he could have had the young woman for his housekeeper and no one would have bothered.

But what the devil, then, was his brother to do? He had been offered, as a sop in the pan, and to keep him quiet, no doubt, over the affair of the Department of Statistics, a vice-consulate in some Mediterranean port﻿—Toulon or Leghorn, or something of the sort. That might have done well enough. It was absurd to think of a Tietjens, heir to Groby, being under the necessity of making a living. It was fantastic, but if Christopher was in a fantastic mood there was nothing to be done about it. A vice-consulate is a potty sort of job. You attend to ships’ manifests, get members of crews out of jail, give old lady tourists the addresses of boardinghouses kept by English or half-castes, or provide the vice-admirals of visiting British squadrons with the names of local residents who should be invited to entertainments given on the flagship. It was a potty job; innocuous too, if it could be regarded as a sort of marking time.﻿ ﻿… And at that moment Mark thought that Christopher was still holding out for some sort of concession on Mark’s part before definitely assuming the charge of Groby, its tenants and its mineral rights.﻿ ﻿… But there were insuperable objections to even the vice-consulate. In the first place, the job would have been in the public service, a fact to which, as has been said, Mark strongly objected. Then the job was offered as a sort of a bribe. And, in addition, the consular service exacts from everyone who occupies a consular or vice-consular post the deposit of a sum of four hundred pounds sterling, and Christopher did not possess even so much as four hundred shillings.﻿ ﻿… And, in addition, as Mark was well aware, Miss Wannop might again afford an obstacle. A British vice-consul might possibly keep a Maltese or Levantine in a back street and no harm done, but he probably could not live with an English young woman of family and position without causing so much scandal as to make him lose his job.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
他知道，比如说，克里斯托弗既是一位颇有分量的数学家，又是国教徒。他大可以领受圣职，去掌马克有权任命的三处家族圣职（livings，教区俸禄）之一，一面称职地履行牧灵义务，一面随心从事一个受人供养的数学家该有的种种营生。

然而克里斯托弗虽坦言偏爱那样的生活——在马克看来，那生活同他的苦修癖、他一身的软心肠和他的私人趣味正相合——也承认有一重障碍，不容他就任这样一份牧灵之职——一重无法逾越的障碍。马克当即问他：是不是其实正同万诺普小姐同居？克里斯托弗却答道，自第二次开赴前线那日起，他就不曾再见过万诺普小姐。他们当时已说定，两人都不是搞偷偷摸摸私情的人，事情就到此为止。

不过马克明白，像克里斯托弗这般心思的人，尽管不曾诱奸一位年轻姑娘，私下却渴望同她结不正当的关系，便很可能自觉无法就任牧灵之职；而这也足以让他有理由说：存在一重无法逾越的障碍。他自己是否赞同，说不准；但教堂里的事，谁同他自己的良心之间的事，他无权置喙。他自己算不得多好的基督徒——至少在男女关系上如此。话虽如此，英格兰国教终归是英格兰国教。毫无疑问，克里斯托弗若是个教皇派，尽可以把那位姑娘收做管家妇，谁也不会多事。

可是，见鬼，那他兄弟到底能干什么呢？人家给他安排过一个地中海港口的副领事——土伦（Toulon）或里窝那（Leghorn）之类——算是安抚，无疑也是堵他的嘴，免得统计部那桩事他再闹。那本也尽够了。一个蒂金斯家的人、格罗比的继承人，居然到了要谋生计的地步，这想法本身就荒唐。荒唐归荒唐，克里斯托弗若犯起荒唐脾气来，谁也没办法。副领事是份无关痛痒的小差事。管管船舶舱单，把闯祸的船员从牢里保出来，给老年女游客介绍英国人或混血儿开的寄宿公寓的地址，或者给来访英国分舰队的海军中将们开列名单——哪些地方人士该请去旗舰上的宴会。小差事一份；也算无害——倘若把它看作一种原地踏步的话。……而那时马克还当克里斯托弗仍在吊价，等他让几分步，才肯正式接手掌管格罗比、它的佃户和矿产权。……可就连这个副领事，也有无法逾越的障碍。第一，这差事属于公职——如前所述，马克对此强烈反对。第二，这差事是当贿赂送来的。再者，领事系统要求凡任领事或副领事之职者，缴存四百英镑保证金，而克里斯托弗连四百先令都拿不出。……还有——这一点马克清楚——万诺普小姐又是一道障碍。英国副领事在后街养个马耳他女人或黎凡特女人，兴许无伤大雅；可他若同一位有家世有身份的英国姑娘同居，闹出的风波十有八九要叫他丢掉差事。……

===Original===
It was at this point that Mark again, but for the last time, asked his brother why he did not divorce Sylvia.

By that time Marie Léonie had retired to get some rest. She was pretty worn out. Mark’s illness had been long and serious; she had nursed him with such care that during the whole time she had not been out into the streets except once or twice to go across the road to the Catholic church, where she would offer a candle or so for his recovery, and once or twice to remonstrate with the butcher as to the quality of the meat he supplied for Mark’s broths. In addition, on many days, she had worked late, under Mark’s directions, on papers that the Office had sent him. She either could not or would not put her man into the charge of any kind of night nurse. She alleged that the war had mopped up every kind of available attendant on the sick, but Mark shrewdly suspected that she had made no kind of effort to secure an assistant. There was her national dread of draughts to account for that. She accepted with discipline, if with despair, the English doctor’s dictum that fresh air must be admitted to the sickroom, but she sat up night after night in a hooded chair, watching for any change in the wind and moving in accordance a complicated arrangement of screens that she maintained between her patient and the open window. She had, however, surrendered Mark to his brother without a murmur, and had quietly gone to her own room to sleep, and Mark, though he carried on almost every kind of conversation with his brother, and though he would not have asked her to leave them in order that he might engage on topics that his brother might like to regard as private﻿—Mark seized the opportunity to lay before Christopher what he thought of Sylvia and the relationships of that singular couple.

It amounted, in the end, to the fact that Mark wanted Christopher to divorce his wife, and to the fact that Christopher had not altered in his views that a man cannot divorce a woman. Mark put it that if Christopher intended to take up with Valentine, it mattered practically very little whether after an attempt at a divorce he married her or not. What a man has to do if he means to take up with a woman, and as far as possible to honour her, is to make some sort of fight of it﻿—as a symbol. Marriage, if you do not regard it as a sacrament﻿—as, no doubt, it ought to be regarded﻿—was nothing more than a token that a couple intended to stick to each other. Nowadays people﻿—the right people﻿—bothered precious little about anything but that. A constant change of partners was a social nuisance; you could not tell whether you could or couldn’t invite a couple together to a tea-fight. And society existed for social functions. That was why promiscuity was no good. For social functions you had to have an equal number of men and women, or someone got left out of conversations, and so you had to know who, officially in the social sense, went with whom. Everyone knew that all the children of Lupus at the War Office were really the children of a Prime Minister, so that presumably the Countess and the Prime Minister slept together most of the time, but that did not mean that you invited the Prime Minister and the woman to social-official functions, because they hadn’t any ostensible token of union. On the contrary, you invited Lord and Lady Lupus together to all functions that would get into the papers, but you took care to have the Lady at any private, weekendish parties or intimate dinners to which the Chief was coming.

===Chinese===
正是在这个节骨眼上，马克再次——也是最后一次——问兄弟：为什么不跟西尔维娅离婚。

那时玛丽·莱昂妮已经退下去歇息了。她也真累得够呛。马克的病拖得又长又重；她照料得那样尽心，整段日子里竟没上过街，只偶尔一两回过马路去天主教堂，为他点上蜡烛求康复；再偶尔一两回去找肉铺老板理论，嫌他供给马克做肉汤的肉成色太次。此外，有许多天，她还在马克口授下熬到深夜，处理部里送来的文件。要把她的男人交给任何一种夜班看护，她要么是不能、要么是不肯。她推说战争把各样能伺候病人的都搜刮光了；可马克思亮地疑心，她压根儿没费一点劲去找帮手。她那法兰西国粹式的穿堂风恐惧症，便是缘故。英国医生断言病房必须放进新鲜空气，她带着纪律性接受了——如果说还带着绝望的话；可她夜复一夜坐在一把带罩的高背椅里守着，留心风向的任何变化，随之挪动那套架在病人和敞开的窗子之间的复杂屏风阵。不过，她把马克交给他兄弟时一声咕哝也没有，安安静静回自己房里睡了；而马克，虽同兄弟几乎无话不谈，虽绝没有请她走开、好谈些兄弟也许愿当作隐私的话题的意思——马克还是抓住机会，把他对西尔维娅的看法、对那古怪两口子关系的看法，一股脑摊给了克里斯托弗。

到头来，事情归结为：马克要克里斯托弗同妻子离婚；而克里斯托弗依旧坚持他的看法——男人不能休妻。马克的说法是：克里斯托弗既打算同瓦伦丁同居，那么打一场离婚官司之后娶不娶她，实际上无甚要紧。一个男人若要同一个女人同居、并尽可能给她体面，该做的是为之打上一仗——作为一个象征。婚姻，倘若你不把它看作圣事——无疑本该看作圣事——便不过是一对男女打算相守到底的信物。如今的人——体面的人——除这个之外，对什么都难得计较。频频更换伴侣是社交上的麻烦：你弄不清一对男女能不能一起请来吃茶会。而社交圈正是为社交活动存在的。正因如此，滥交才要不得。办社交活动，男女人数必须相等，不然总有人插不进谈话；所以你必须知道，按社交的官面说法，谁同谁是一路。人人都知道陆军部卢普斯（Lupus）家的孩子其实全是某位首相的种，可见那位伯爵夫人同首相多半时候是同床的；可这并不意味着你会把首相同那位夫人一同请去官式社交活动，因为他们没有公开的结合信物。恰恰相反，凡是见报的活动，你都请卢普斯勋爵夫妇同来；可凡是首相莅临的私人周末小聚或至交晚宴，你总要留神把卢普斯夫人请到。

===Original===
And Christopher had to consider that if it came to marriage ninety percent of the inhabitants of the world regarded the marriages of almost everybody else as invalid. A Papist obviously could not regard a marriage before an English registrar or a French *maire* as having any moral validity. At best it was no more than a demonstration of aspirations after constancy. You went before a functionary publicly to assert that man and woman intended to stick to each other. Equally for extreme Protestants a marriage by a Papist priest, or a minister of any other sect, or a Buddhist Lama, had not the blessing of their own brand of Deity. So that really, to all practical intents, it was sufficient if a couple really assured their friends that they intended to stick together, if possible, forever; if not, at least for years enough to show that they had made a good shot at it. Mark invited Christopher to consult whom he liked in his, Mark’s, particular set and he would find that they agreed with his views.

So he was anxious that if Christopher intended to take up with the Wannop young woman he should take at least a shot at a divorce. He might not succeed in getting one. He obviously had grounds enough, but Sylvia might make counter-allegations, he, Mark, couldn’t say with what chance of success. He was prepared himself to accept his brother’s assertions of complete innocence, but Sylvia was a clever devil and there was no knowing what view a judge might take. Where there had been such a hell of a lot of smoke he might consider that there must be enough flame to justify refusing a divorce. There would no doubt be, thus﻿—a beastly stink. But a beastly stink would be better than the sort of veiled ill-fame that Sylvia had contrived to get attached to Christopher. And the fact that Christopher had faced the stink and made the attempt would be at least that amount of tribute to Miss Wannop. Society was good-natured and was inclined to take the view that if a fellow had faced his punishment and taken it he was pretty well absolved. There might be people who would hold out against them, but Mark supposed that what Christopher wanted for himself and his girl was reasonable material comfort with a society of sufficient people of the right sort to give them a dinner or so a week and a weekend or so a month in the weekending season.

Christopher had acquiesced in the justness of his views with so much amiability that Mark began to hope that he would get his way in the larger matter of Groby. He was prepared to go further and to stake as much as his assurance that if Christopher would settle down at Groby, accept a decent income and look after the estate, he, Mark, would assure his brother and Valentine of bearable social circumstances.

===Chinese===
还有一层，克里斯托弗必须考虑：一旦谈到结婚，世界上百分之九十的居民，都把几乎其他所有人的婚姻看作无效。教皇派信徒显然不会认为，在英国登记官或法国镇长（maire）面前缔结的婚姻有什么道德效力；至多不过是一种对坚贞的公开向往罢了——你走到一名官吏面前，公开宣告一对男女打算相守。同样，在极端新教徒眼里，天主教神父、任何别一教派的牧师、或佛教喇嘛主持的婚姻，也得不到他们那一款神的祝福。所以，就一切实际效用而言，一对男女只要当真向朋友们担保打算相守——可能的话，相守到老；不能的话，至少相守足够多的年头，证明这一回他们认真试过——也就够了。马克请克里斯托弗去问他马克那个圈子里随便哪一个人，他会发现人人都同意他的见解。

所以他一心盼着：克里斯托弗既打算同万诺普家那姑娘同居，至少也该朝离婚放上一枪。也许打不中。理由他显然绰绰有余，可西尔维娅兴许会反诉，成败几何，他马克说不准。他自己尽可采信兄弟自称完全清白的说法，可西尔维娅是个精明的魔鬼，法官会怎么看，谁也说不准。既然烟雾闹过这么大、这么久，法官也许会认定火头必也够旺，足以驳回一纸离婚。这样一来，无疑要有一场难看的臭风波。可一场臭风波，总强过西尔维娅处心积虑安到克里斯托弗头上那种半遮半掩的恶名。而克里斯托弗敢面对这场臭风波、敢做这一番尝试，至少对万诺普小姐算是一份敬意。社交界素来心肠好，倾向于认为：一个人既敢面对惩罚、又领了惩罚，也就洗得差不多了。也许还有人死咬着不松口；不过马克想，克里斯托弗为自己和姑娘要的，不过是像样的物质安逸，外加一个够分量的体面圈子——每周请他们吃一两顿饭，周末社交季里每月请他们过一两个周末。

克里斯托弗和颜悦色地认可了这些见解的公道，马克便开始指望，在格罗比这桩大事上也能照他的意思办。他准备更进一步，近乎打保票地说：只要克里斯托弗肯在格罗比安顿下来、领一份体面的进项、照管庄园，他马克就保克里斯托弗和瓦伦丁一个过得去的社交处境。

===Original===
Christopher, however, had made no answer at all beyond saying that if he tried to divorce Sylvia it would apparently ruin his old-furniture business. For his American partner assured him that in the United States if a man divorced his wife instead of letting her divorce him no one would do any business with him. He had mentioned the case of a man called Blum, a pretty warm stock-exchange man, who insisted on divorcing his wife against the advice of his friends; he found when he returned to the stock market that all his clients cold-shouldered him, so that he was ruined. And as these fellows were shortly going to mop up everything in the world, including the old-furniture trade, Christopher supposed that he would have to study their prejudices.

He had come across his partner rather curiously. The fellow, whose father had been a German-Jew but a naturalized American citizen, had been in Berlin mopping up German old furniture for sale in the American interior, where he had a flourishing business. So, when America had come in on the side that was not German, the Germans had just simply dropped on Mr. Schatzweiler in their pleasant way, incorporated him in their forces and had sent him to the front as a miserable little Tommy before the Americans had been a month in the show. And there, amongst the prisoners he had had to look after, Christopher had found the little, large-eyed sensitive creature, unable to speak a word of German but just crazy about the furniture and tapestries in the French châteaux that the prisoners passed on their marches. Christopher had befriended him; kept him as far as possible separated from the other prisoners, who naturally did not like him, and had a good many conversations with him.

It had appeared that Mr. Schatzweiler had had a good deal to do in the way of buying with Sir John Robertson, the old old-furniture millionaire, who was a close friend of Sylvia’s and had been so considerable admirer of Christopher’s furniture-buying gifts that he had, years ago, proposed to take Christopher into partnership with himself. At that time Christopher had regarded Sir John’s proposals as outside the range of his future; he had then been employed in the Department of Statistics. But the proposal had always amused and rather impressed him. If, that is to say, that hardheaded old Scotsman who had made a vast fortune at his trade made to Christopher a quite serious business proposition on the strength of Christopher’s flair in the matter of old woods and curves, Christopher himself might take his own gifts with a certain seriousness.

===Chinese===
然而克里斯托弗的回答只有一句：他若试图同西尔维娅离婚，显然会毁了他的旧家具生意。因为那位美国合伙人向他担保：在美国，一个男人若是自己休妻、而不是让妻子休他，就没人肯同他做生意。他举过一个叫布卢姆（Blum）的人做例子——个手头相当阔绰的证券交易所客——不顾朋友劝阻，执意休了妻；回到证券市场才发现，主顾们一齐冷落他，就此毁了。而这伙人不久就要把世上的一切、连旧家具行当在内，尽数吸进腰包；克里斯托弗想，他们的偏见，他不能不迁就。

他遇上这位合伙人的经过颇为离奇。这家伙的父亲是德裔犹太人，不过入了美国籍；他本人战时正在柏林为美国内地市场搜购德国家具——在内地他有一桩兴隆的生意。于是，美国一参战、站到德国的对头那边，德国人便以他们那讨人喜欢的方式收拾了沙茨韦勒先生：把他编进军队，等美国人进场还不满一个月，就把他当个可怜巴巴的小卒子送上了前线。就在前线，在克里斯托弗看守的俘虏中间，他发现了这个大眼睛、神经质的小家伙——一句德语不会说，行军路过法国城堡时，却对城里的家具和织锦挂毯如痴如狂。

原来，沙茨韦勒先生同老家具业百万富翁约翰·罗伯逊爵士颇有采购上的往来；罗伯逊爵士是西尔维娅的密友，又一向极赏识克里斯托弗买家具的眼力，多年前就提议让克里斯托弗同他合伙。那时克里斯托弗只当爵士的提议同自己前程无涉——他当时还在统计部任职。但这提议一直叫他觉得有趣，也颇受抬举。这就是说：那样一个精明头脑、在本行里挣下偌大家业的老苏格兰人，竟凭克里斯托弗辨识老木料与曲线的眼力，向他郑重其事地提过一桩正经生意——那么克里斯托弗本人，对自己的这份天赋，也不妨当真几分。

===Original===
And by the time he came to be in command of the escort over those miserable creatures he had pretty well realized that after the necessity for escorts was over he would jolly well have to consider how he was going to make a living for himself. That was certain. He was not going to reinsert himself amongst the miserable collection of squits who occupied themselves in his old Department; he was too old to continue in the Army; he was certainly not going to accept a penny from Groby sources. He did not care what became of him﻿—but his not caring did not take any tragico-romantic form. He would be quite prepared to live in a hut on a hillside and cook his meals over three bricks outside the door﻿—but that was not a method of life that was very practicable and even that needed money. Everyone who served in the Army at the front knew how little it took to keep life going﻿—and satisfactory. But he did not see the world, when it settled down again, turning itself into a place fit for old soldiers who had learned to appreciate frugality. On the contrary, the old soldiers would be chivvied to hell by a civilian population who abhorred them. So that merely to keep clean and out of debt was going to be a tough job.

So, in his long vigils in tents, beneath the moon, with the sentries walking, challenging from time to time, round the barbed wire stockades, the idea of Sir John’s proposition had occurred to him with some force. It had gathered strength from his meeting with Mr. Schatzweiler. The little fellow was a shivering artist, and Christopher had enough of superstition in him to be impressed by the coincidence of their having come together in such unlikely circumstances. After all, Providence must let up on him after a time, so why should not this unfortunate and impressively Oriental member of the Chosen People be a sign of a covenant? In a way he reminded Christopher of his former protégé Macmaster﻿—he had the same dark eyes, the same shape, the same shivering eagerness.

That he was a Jew and an American did not worry Christopher; he had not objected to the fact that Macmaster had been the son of a Scotch grocer. If he had to go into partnership and be thrown into close contact with anyone at all he did not care much who it was as long as it was not either a bounder or a man of his own class and race. To be in close mental communion with either an English bounder or an Englishman of good family would, he was aware, be intolerable to him. But, for a little shivering, artistic Jew, as of old for Macmaster, he was quite capable of feeling a real fondness﻿—as you might for an animal. Their manners were not your manners and could not be expected to be, and whatever their intelligences, they would have a certain little alertness, a certain exactness of thought.﻿ ﻿… Besides, if they did you in, as every business partner or protégé must be expected to do, you did not feel the same humiliation as you did if you were swindled by a man of your own race and station. In the one case it was only what was to be expected, in the other you were faced with the fact that your own tradition had broken down. And under the long strain of the war he had outgrown alike the mentality and the traditions of his own family and his own race. The one and the other were not fitted to endure long strains.

===Chinese===
等到他来统带押送那伙可怜家伙的队伍时，他心里已相当明白：押送的差事一完，他可就得好好思量自个儿靠什么谋生了。这是肯定的。他不打算再挤回旧部里那一群无聊的蠢货中间去；他年纪太大，续不了军职；格罗比来路的一个子儿，他肯定不会领。他不在乎自己落个什么下场——但他这份不在乎，并不带半点悲壮浪漫的腔调。他满可以住山坡上一间窝棚，在门口三块架起的砖上烧饭——可这种活法并不大行得通，就连它也要钱。在前线当过兵的人都知道，维持生计——而且维持得心满意足——所需的何其少。可他不认为，世界一旦安定下来，会把自己变成一个适于学会安贫的老兵们生存的地方。恰恰相反，老兵们会被憎恶他们的平民百姓追打得鸡飞狗跳。所以说，光是保持干净、不欠债，就已是一桩苦差了。

于是，在月下帐篷里的漫长守夜中，听着哨兵绕着铁丝网围栏巡行、时时喝问口令，约翰爵士那桩提议带着几分力量回到了他心头。同沙茨韦勒先生的相遇，又给它添了分量。那小家伙是个瑟瑟发抖的艺术家，而克里斯托弗骨子里迷信够深，两人的相遇竟凑巧到这般不可思议的地步，不由他不为之动容。说到底，上苍也该有饶过他的一天吧？那么，上帝选民里这位倒霉而仪表堂堂的东方人，为什么就不能是一道圣约的记号呢？有几分上，他让克里斯托弗想起旧日的门生麦克马斯特（Macmaster）——同样的黑眼睛，同样的身形，同样瑟瑟发抖的热切。

他是犹太人又是美国人，这并不碍克里斯托弗的事；麦克马斯特是苏格兰杂货商的儿子，他当年也不曾嫌过。既然要合伙、要同人朝夕共事，对方是谁他不大在乎，只要不是个粗俗的暴发户，也不是他本阶级本族的人就行。同一个英国粗人、或同一个世家出身的英国人心神相通地共处，他知道，那对自己是无法忍受的。可对一个瑟瑟发抖的小小犹太艺术家——一如当年对麦克马斯特——他完全可能生出一份真切的喜爱——就像你疼一头牲口那样。他们的礼数不是你的礼数，也不能指望是；无论他们智力如何，总带着几分小机灵，几分思路的精确。……况且，倘若他们坑了你——合伙人也好，门生也好，坑你本是料中应有之事——那滋味同被本族本阶层的人坑骗大不一样。前一种情形，本在意料之中；后一种情形，你面对的是自家传统已然崩坏的事实。而在战争的长久重压下，他早已长过了自家家族与自家族裔的心性与传统——两下里都熬不住长久的重压。

===Original===
So he welcomed the imploring glances and the eventual Oriental gratitude of that little man in his unhappy tent. For, naturally, by communicating in his weighty manner with the United States Headquarters when he happened to find himself in its vicinity, he secured the release of the little fellow, who was by now safely back somewhere in the interior of the North American Continent.

But before that happened he had exchanged a certain amount of correspondence with Sir John, and had discovered from him and from one or two chance members of the American Expeditionary Force that the little man was quite a good old-furniture dealer. Sir John had by that time gone out of business and his letters were not particularly cordial to Tietjens﻿—which was only what was to be expected if Sylvia had been shedding her charms over him. But it had appeared that Mr. Schatzweiler had had a great deal of business with Sir John, who had indeed supplied him with a great part of his material, and so, if Sir John had gone out of business, Mr. Schatzweiler would need to find in England someone to take Sir John’s place. And that was not going to be extraordinarily easy, for what with the amount of his money that the Germans had mopped up﻿—they had sold him immense quantities of old furniture and got paid for it, and had then enlisted him in the ranks of their Brandenburgers, where naturally he could do nothing with carved oak chests that had elaborate steel hinges and locks.﻿ ﻿… What then with that, and his prolonged absence from the neighbourhood of Detroit, where he had mostly found his buyers, Mr. Schatzweiler found himself extremely hampered in his activities. It therefore fell to Christopher, if he was to go into partnership with the now sanguine and charming Oriental, to supply an immediate sum of money. That had not been easy, but by means of mortgaging his pay and his blood-money, and selling the books that Sylvia had left him, he had been able to provide Mr. Schatzweiler with enough to make at least a start somewhere across the water.﻿ ﻿… And Mr. Schatzweiler and Christopher had between them evolved an ingenious scheme along lines that the American had long contemplated, taking into account the tastes of his countrymen and the nature of the times.

Mark had listened to his brother during all this with indulgence and even with pleasure. If a Tietjens contemplated going into trade he might at least contemplate an amusing trade carried on in a spirited manner. And what Christopher humorously projected was at least more dignified than stock-broking or bill-discounting. Moreover, he was pretty well convinced by this time that his brother was completely reconciled to him and to Groby.

===Chinese===
但在那之前，他同约翰爵士通了几回信，并从爵士和美国远征军（American Expeditionary Force）里一两个偶然结识的成员口中得知：这小家伙是相当在行的旧家具商。约翰爵士那时已经歇业，给蒂金斯的信写得也不怎么热络——倘若西尔维娅曾对他施过魅力，这本也意料中事。但据说沙茨韦勒先生同约翰爵士生意往来极多，爵士的货有相当一部分正是供他的；因此爵士既已歇业，沙茨韦勒先生便需要在英格兰另找一个人，接爵士的班。而这绝非易事：一来，德国人搜刮了他大笔的钱——他们卖给他大宗大宗的旧家具、收足了货款，然后把他征进勃兰登堡部队的行伍；在那儿，带着精钢铰链锁具的雕花橡木箱，自然一件也派不上用场。……再加上他长期离开底特律（Detroit）一带——他的买主多半在那里——沙茨韦勒先生发觉自己的营生处处束手束脚。于是，克里斯托弗若要同这位如今满怀憧憬、招人喜欢的东方人合伙，就得拿出一笔立等可用的钱来。这谈何容易；可他抵押了军饷和卖命钱，又卖掉西尔维娅留下的那几本书，总算凑足了让沙茨韦勒先生至少在大洋彼岸先起步的数目。……沙茨韦勒先生和克里斯托弗还据此共同琢磨出一套精明的方案——顺着那美国人酝酿已久的思路，把他同胞的口味和时势的性质都算了进去。

这一大篇话，马克听得纵容，甚至愉快。一个蒂金斯家的人既打算经商，至少也该经营一门有趣的、干起来有声有色的行当。克里斯托弗带着幽默筹划的这门营生，至少比做证券经纪或票据贴现体面。何况，到这时他已相当确信：兄弟对他、对格罗比，都已完全解怨和好。

于是，他在那顶倒霉的帐篷里，欣然领受了那小家伙哀求的眼神，以及后来那份东方人的感激。因为，自然，每逢恰好行军行到美军司令部附近，他便以他那郑重其事的做派同司令部通声气，终于给小家伙办到了释放——如今他已平安回到北美大陆腹地的某个地方了。

===Original===
It was about then and when he had again begun to introduce the topic of Groby that Christopher got up from the chair at the bedside that he had been occupying and, having taken his brother’s wrist in his cool fingers, remarked:

“Your temperature’s pretty well down. Don’t you think it is about time that you set about marrying Charlotte? I suppose you mean to marry her before this bout is finished; you might have a relapse.”

Mark remembered that speech perfectly well, with the addition that if he, Christopher, hurried about it they might get the job done that night. It must therefore then have been about one o’clock of an afternoon about three weeks before the 11th November, 1918.

Mark had replied that he would be much obliged to Christopher, and Christopher, having roused Marie Léonie and told her that he would be back in time to let her have a good night’s rest, disappeared, saying that he was going straight to Lambeth. In those days, supposing you could command thirty pounds or so, there was no difficulty in getting married at the shortest possible notice, and Christopher had promoted too many last-minute marriages amongst his men not to know the ropes.

Mark viewed the transaction with a good deal of satisfaction. It had needed no arguing; if the proceeding had the approval of the heir-presumptive to Groby there was nothing more to be said against it. And Mark took the view that if he agreed to a proceeding that Christopher could only have counselled as heir-presumptive, that was an additional reason for Mark’s expecting that Christopher would eventually consent to administer Groby himself.

===Chinese===
大约就在那时，他又刚起头要引回格罗比的话头，克里斯托弗从床边他坐着的椅子里站起身，用他冰凉的手指握住兄弟的手腕，说道：

「你的体温差不多降下来了。你不觉得，该着手把夏洛特娶进门了吗？我想你总打算趁这趟病没完先把婚结了；万一复发呢。」

这句话马克记得清清楚楚，连同那后半句：他克里斯托弗若手脚麻利些，当晚就能把事情办妥。所以那时准是下午一点钟光景，日期约在 1918 年 11 月 11 日之前三个星期。

马克回答，那就多谢克里斯托弗费心了。克里斯托弗唤醒玛丽·莱昂妮，告诉她自己会及时赶回、让她安睡一夜，随后人就没了踪影，只说这就直奔兰贝斯（Lambeth）。在那个年头，只要拿得出三十镑上下，想在最短的通知期限内结婚，毫无难处；克里斯托弗在手下士兵们中间促成的临阵婚礼太多了，这套门道他熟得很。

马克对这桩交易相当满意。这事用不着争辩；这桩事既得格罗比假定继承人（heir-presumptive）的首肯，再有什么可指摘的，也就无话可说了。而马克的看法是：既然他应允了一件克里斯托弗只能以假定继承人身份来谏议的事，这就多了一条理由，可以指望克里斯托弗终有一天肯亲自掌管格罗比。

### VI / 第六章

===Original===
That would have been three weeks before the eleventh of November. His mind boggled a little at computing what the actual date in October must have been. With his then pneumonia his mind had not much registered the dates of that period; days had gone by in fever and boredom. Still, a man ought to remember the date of his wedding. Say it had been the twentieth of October, 1918. The twentieth of October had been his father’s birthday. When he came to think of it he could remember remembering hazily that it was queer that he should be going out of life on the date his father had entered it. It made a sort of full stop. And it made a full stop that, practically on that day, Papists entered into their own in Groby. He had, that is to say, made up his mind to the fact that Christopher’s son would have Groby as a home even if Christopher didn’t. And the boy was by now a full-fledged Papist, pickled and oiled and wafered and all. Sylvia had rubbed the fact in about a week ago by sending him a card for his nephew’s provisional baptism and first communion about a week before. It had astonished him that he had not felt more bitter.

He had not any doubt that the fact had reconciled him to his marriage with Marie Léonie. He had told his brother a year or so before that he would never marry her because she was a Papist, but he was aware that then he was only chipping at Spelden, the fellow that wrote *Spelden on Sacrilege*, a book that predicted all sorts of disaster for fellows who owned former Papist Church lands or who had displaced Papists. When he had told Christopher that he would never marry Charlotte﻿—he had called her Charlotte for reasons of camouflage before the marriage﻿—he had been quite aware that he was chipping at Spelden’s ghost﻿—for Spelden must have been dead a hundred years or so. As it were, he had been saying grimly if pleasantly to that bogey:

“Eh, old un. You see. You may prophesy disaster to Groby because a Tietjens was given it over the head of one of your fellows in Dutch William’s time. But you can’t frighten me into making an honest woman﻿—let alone a Lady of Groby﻿—out of a Papist.”

And he hadn’t. He would swear that no idea of disaster to Groby had entered his head at the date of the marriage. Now, he would not say; but of what he felt then he was certain. He remembered thinking whilst the ceremony was going on of the words of Fraser of Lovat before they executed him in the Forty-Five. They had told him on the scaffold that if he would make some sort of submission to George II they would spare his body from being exhibited in quarters on the spikes of the buildings in Edinburgh. And Fraser had answered: “An the King will have my heid I care not what he may do with my —﻿—﻿—,” naming a part of a gentleman that is not now mentioned in drawing-rooms. So, if a Papist was to inhabit Groby House, it mattered precious little if the first Lady Tietjens of Groby were Papist or Heathen.

===Chinese===
那该是 11 月 11 日之前三个星期。要算出 10 月里的确切日子该是哪一天，他的脑子有点儿转不过来。那阵子他害着肺炎，那些日子的日期在脑子里没大留下；一天天都在发热和无趣里过去。话虽如此，一个男人总该记得自己结婚的日子。就算那是 1918 年 10 月 20 日吧。10 月 20 日原是他父亲的生日。如今想来，他还记得自己当时就恍恍惚惚地琢磨过：他将在父亲走进人世的那一天走出人世，这真是奇了。这算是一个句点。而这个句点还连着另一层：差不多就在那一天，教皇派（Papist，英格兰新教徒对天主教徒的旧时贬称）在格罗比得了势。也就是说，他已然想定了：克里斯托弗的儿子要把格罗比当作家园，哪怕克里斯托弗自己不要。那孩子如今已是羽毛丰满的教皇派，腌透了、膏了圣油、领了圣饼，全套齐备。西尔维娅大约一周前、也就是典礼前约一周，寄来一张帖子，请他去参加侄子的暂行洗礼（provisional baptism，天主教对已在别教受洗者的重新施洗）和初领圣餐，把这事实狠狠戳给他看。他自己竟没觉得更苦几分，这倒叫他吃了一惊。

他毫不怀疑，正是这个事实让他安于同玛丽·莱昂妮的婚姻。一年多以前，他跟弟弟说过，他绝不会娶她，因为她是教皇派；可他心里明白，那时他不过是拿斯佩尔登（Spelden）开涮——就是写《斯佩尔登论渎圣》（*Spelden on Sacrilege*）的那家伙。那部书预言：凡占着昔日教皇派教产、或挤掉了教皇派位子的人，各样的灾祸都要落到头上。他告诉克里斯托弗说自己绝不娶夏洛特（Charlotte）的时候——婚前为了遮人耳目，他一直管她叫夏洛特——他心里清清楚楚，自己是在戳斯佩尔登的鬼魂——因为斯佩尔登总归死了百来年了。那情形，不啻是对那个老妖怪板着脸又不失和气地说：

「唉，老家伙。你瞧。荷兰威廉（Dutch William）那会儿，一个蒂金斯越过你家哪个人的头顶得了格罗比，你尽管为此预言灾殃。可你吓不倒我，我偏不把一个教皇派扶成良家妇人——更别提扶成格罗比夫人了。」

而他也确实没有被吓倒。他可以起誓：行婚礼那阵子，格罗比遭灾的念头压根儿没进过他的脑子。如今嘛，他可就不肯说了；可他当时是什么心情，他记得确确的。他记得，仪式进行当中，他想起洛瓦特的弗雷泽（Fraser of Lovat）在四五年之乱（the Forty-Five，1745 年詹姆士党起事）中被处决前说的话。人家在行刑台上告诉他，只要肯向乔治二世稍稍服个软，就免了他的尸身被剁作几块、挂上爱丁堡各处建筑的铁签示众。弗雷泽答道：「俺的脑袋国王要拿去便拿去，他才拿俺的——咋办，俺才不放在心上，」点的那件东西，是绅士身上一处如今客厅里提不得的部位。所以说，反正有个教皇派要住进格罗比大宅，格罗比头一位蒂金斯夫人是教皇派还是异教徒，也就稀松得很了。

===Original===
A man as a rule does not marry his mistress whilst he has any kick in him. If he still aims at a career it might hinder him if she were known to have been his mistress, or, of course, a fellow who wants to make a career might want to help himself on by making a good marriage. Even if a man does not want to make a career he may think that a woman who has been his mistress as like as not may cuckold him after marriage, for, if she has gone wrong with him, she would be more apt to go wrong elsewhere as well. But if a fellow is practically finished those considerations disappear, and he remembers that you go to hell if you seduce virgins. It is as well at one time or another to make your peace with your Creator. Forever is a long word and God is said to disapprove of unconsecrated unions.

Besides, it would very likely please Marie Léonie, though she had never said a word about it, and it would certainly dish Sylvia, who was no doubt counting on being the first Lady Tietjens of Groby. And then, too, it would undoubtedly make Marie Léonie safer. In one way and another he had given his mistress quite a number of things that might well be desirable to that bitch, and neither his nor Christopher’s lives were worth much, whilst Chancery can be a very expensive affair if you get into it.

And he was aware that he had always had a soft spot in his heart for Marie Léonie, otherwise he would not have provided her with the name of Charlotte for public consumption. A man gives his mistress another name if there is any chance of his marrying her, so that it may look as if he were marrying someone else when he does it. *Marie Léonie Riotor* looks different from a casual Charlotte. It gives her a better chance in the world outside.

So it had been well enough. The world was changing and there was no particular reason why he should not change with it.﻿ ﻿… And he had not been able to conceal from himself that he was getting on the way. Time lengthened out. When he had come in drenched from one of the potty local meetings that they had had to fall back on during the war he had known that something was coming to him, because after Marie Léonie had tucked him up in bed he could not remember the strain of the winner of some handicap of no importance. Marie Léonie had given him a goodish tot of rum with butter in it and that might have made him hazy﻿—but, all the same, that had never happened to him in his life before, rum or no rum. And by now he had forgotten even the name of the winner and the meeting.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
一个男人只要身上还有半点儿精气神，照例是不会娶自己情妇的。倘若他还图着前程，让世人知道她做过自己的情妇，兴许要碍事；再不然，一个想往上爬的人，兴许还想靠一门好亲事抬举自己。就算不图前程，他也会想：做过他情妇的女人，婚后十有八九要给他戴绿帽——既然跟他肯失身，跟了别人失身也就更容易。可一个人若是差不多完了，这些盘算便都散了，他倒记起：诱奸处女是要下地狱的。早晚总得跟造物主讲和一回。「永远」是个分量沉重的字眼，况且据说上帝不喜悦未经祝圣的结合。

再说，这多半会让玛丽·莱昂妮欢喜，虽说她一个字也没提过；这也准能将西尔维娅一军——她无疑正指望着当格罗比头一位蒂金斯夫人。还有，这也毫无疑问能让玛丽·莱昂妮更安稳。七七八八算下来，他给了自己情妇不少东西，桩桩件件都是那条母狗眼馋的；而他这条命也好，克里斯托弗那条命也好，都不值几个钱，况且一旦缠进大法官法庭（Chancery），那可是个花钱如流水的地方。

他也明白，自己心里对玛丽·莱昂妮终究存着一处软的地方，不然他也不会替她备下「夏洛特」这个名字给外头人叫。一个男人若存着娶情妇的万一之想，就会另给她起个名字，这样一来，真娶的时候倒像是娶了另外一个人。玛丽·莱昂妮·里奥托（*Marie Léonie Riotor*），听着就跟一个随口的夏洛特不一样。这叫她在外头的世上多几分体面。

所以一切也算妥帖。世道在变，他没道理不跟着变。……他也没法瞒过自己：他正在往那条路上去了。日子拉长了。有一回，他从战时只好凑合着去的本地那些不入流赛马会上淋得精湿回来，就知道有什么要落到他头上了，因为玛丽·莱昂妮伺候他躺下、掖好被之后，他竟想不起一场无足轻重的让磅赛里那匹胜马的血统。玛丽·莱昂妮给他灌了一大盅兑黄油的朗姆酒，那兴许把他闹迷糊了——可是话又说回来，他这辈子从没出过这种事，有没有朗姆酒都一样。到如今，连那匹马的名字、那场赛马会，他都忘了个干净。……

===Original===
He could not conceal from himself that his memory was failing, though otherwise he considered himself to be as sound a man as he had ever been. But when it came to memory, ever since that day his brain had checked at times as a tired horse will at a fence.﻿ ﻿… A tired horse!

He could not bring himself to the computation of what three weeks back from the eleventh of November came to; his brain would not go at it. For the matter of that, he could remember precious little of the events of that three weeks in their due order. Christopher had certainly been about, relieving Marie Léonie at night and attending to him with a soft, goggle-eyed attentiveness that only a man with a saint for a mother could have put up. For hours and hours he would read aloud in Boswell’s *Life of Johnson*, for which Mark had had a fancy.

And Mark could remember drowsing off with satisfaction to the sound of the voice and drowsing with satisfaction awake again, still to the sound of the voice. For Christopher had the idea that if his voice went droning on it would make Mark’s slumbers more satisfactory.

Satisfaction.﻿ ﻿… Perhaps the last satisfaction that Mark was ever to know. For at that time﻿—during those three weeks﻿—he had not been able to believe that Christopher really meant to stick out about the matter of Groby. How could you believe that a fellow who waited on you with the softness of a girl built of meal-sacks was determined to﻿ ﻿… call it, break your heart. That was what it came to.﻿ ﻿… A fellow, too, who agreed in the most astounding manner with your views of things in general. A fellow, for the matter of that, who knew ten times as much as you did. A damn learned fellow.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
他没法瞒过自己：记性在坏下去；除此之外，他自认还是一向那样一条结实汉子。可单说记性，打那天起，他的脑子时不时地要打一个绊，像一匹疲马在篱障前猛地收步。……一匹疲马！

从 11 月 11 日往回推三个星期到底是哪一天，他怎么也逼着自己算不出来；他的脑子不肯往那上头去。说实话，那三个星期里的事，按先后次序，他记得的少得可怜。克里斯托弗自然一直在跟前，夜里替下玛丽·莱昂妮，用一种温软的、鼓着一双眼睛的殷勤看护他——那般殷勤，也只有母亲是圣徒的人才拿得出来。他会一连好几个钟头朗读鲍斯威尔的《约翰逊传》（*Life of Johnson*），马克素来偏爱这部书。

马克还记得，自己就着那嗓音心满意足地打起盹来，又心满意足地醒转来，耳边还是那嗓音。因为克里斯托弗有一条心得：他那嗓音只要一直嗡嗡地念下去，马克的睡眠便更满足。

满足。……这兴许是马克此生所知的最后一味满足了。因为那时候——在那三个星期里——他怎么也不能相信，克里斯托弗在格罗比这件事上是当真要犟到底。一个像粮袋堆成的姑娘那般软和地伺候你的人，你怎能相信他打定了主意要……姑且说，撕碎你的心。归结起来就是这么回事。……何况这人附和起你对世事的见解来，附和得叫人咋舌。何况，说真的，他的学问十倍于你。一个他妈的博学之人。……

===Original===
Mark had no contempt for learning﻿—particularly for younger sons. The country was going to the dogs because of the want of education of the younger sons, whose business it was to do the work of the nation. It was a very old North Country rhyme that, that when land is gone and money spent, then learning is most excellent. No, he had no contempt for learning. He had never acquired any because he was too lazy: a little Sallust, a little Cornelius Nepos, a touch of Horace, enough French to read a novel and follow what Marie Léonie said.﻿ ﻿… Even to himself he called her Marie Léonie once he was married to her. It had made her jump at first!

But Christopher was a damn learned fellow. Their father, a younger son at the beginning, had been damn learned, too. They said that even at his death he had been one of the best Latinists in England﻿—the intimate friend of that fellow Wannop, the Professor.﻿ ﻿… A great age at which to die by his own hand, his father’s! Why, if that marriage had been on the 20th October, 1918, his father, then dead, must have been born on the 20th October what?﻿ ﻿… 1834.﻿ ﻿… No, that was not possible.﻿ ﻿… No: ’44. *His* father, Mark knew, had been born in 1812﻿—before Waterloo!

Great stretches of time. Great changes! Yet Father had not been an incult sort of a man. On the contrary, if he was burly and determined, he was quiet. And sensitive. He had certainly loved Christopher very dearly﻿—and Christopher’s mother.

Father was very tall; stooping like a toppling poplar towards the end. His head seemed very distant as if he hardly heard you. Iron-grey; short-whiskered! Absentminded towards the end. Forgetting where he had put his handkerchief and where his spectacles were when he had pushed them up on to his forehead.﻿ ﻿… He had been a younger son who had never spoken to his father for forty years. Father’s father had never forgiven him for marrying Miss Selby of Biggen﻿ ﻿… not because it was marrying below him, but because his father had wanted their mother for his eldest son.﻿ ﻿… And they had been poor in their early childhood, wandering over the Continent, to settle at last in Dijon, where they had kept some sort of state﻿ ﻿… a large house in the middle of the town with several servants. He never could imagine how their mother had done it on four hundred a year. But she had. A hard woman. But Father had kept in with French people and corresponded with Professor Wannop and Learned Societies. He had always regarded him, Mark, as rather a dunce.﻿ ﻿… Father would sit reading in elegantly bound books, by the hour. His study had been one of the showrooms of the house in Dijon.

===Chinese===
马克不轻看学问——尤其不轻看次子们的学问。国家就坏在次子们没受教养上，而全国的实际营生，本就指着次子们去干。北方有一句很老的谣谚：田产尽了，钱财光了，学问才最顶用。不，他不轻看学问。他自己没学到什么，全因为太懒：一点儿萨卢斯特（Sallust），一点儿科尔奈利乌斯·尼波斯（Cornelius Nepos），一星儿贺拉斯（Horace），法文够读小说、够听懂玛丽·莱昂妮说什么。……一经成婚，连他对自己叫她，也是玛丽·莱昂妮了。起初可吓了她一跳！

可克里斯托弗就是个他妈的博学之人。他们的父亲，起先也是次子，也曾他妈的博学。人家说，直到咽气那天，他都还是英格兰数一数二的拉丁文大家——万诺普教授那个家伙的至交。……以那样的高龄亲手了断自己，他们的父亲！嘿，倘若那场婚礼是 1918 年 10 月 20 日，那么他那已故的父亲，准是生在 10 月 20 日，哪一年呢？……1834。……不，这不可能。……不：是 '44 年。而他*父亲的*父亲，马克知道，生于 1812 年——滑铁卢战役之前！

大段大段的岁月。翻天覆地的变迁！话虽如此，父亲却不是个粗陋无文的人。恰恰相反，他虽魁伟专断，却性子安静。而且敏感。他确实极疼克里斯托弗——也疼克里斯托弗的母亲。

父亲身量很高；到末了，像一株将倾的白杨似的伛偻着。他的头显得很远，仿佛不大听得见你说话。铁灰的须发；短短的连鬓胡子！到末了人变得心不在焉。记不得手帕搁在了哪儿，眼镜推上了额头，又记不得眼镜在哪儿。……他做过次子，同自己的父亲四十年不说话。父亲的父亲至死不肯饶他娶了比根（Biggen）的塞尔比（Selby）小姐……不是因为这婚事辱没了门第，而是因为他们的祖父，原想让他们母亲配给自己的长子。……他们幼年家境清寒，漂泊欧陆，最后在第戎（Dijon）落脚，还撑起了几分场面……城中心一座大宅，使着好几个仆人。他永远想不明白，他们的母亲是怎么靠一年四百镑撑下来的。可她就是撑下来了。一个刚硬的女人。不过父亲同法国人一直有来往，又同万诺普教授和各家学会通信。他一向把他马克看作个不折不扣的笨学生。……父亲会一坐几个钟头，捧着装帧雅致的书读。他的书房是第戎那座宅子里的一间门面房。

===Original===
*Did* he commit suicide? If so then Valentine Wannop was his daughter. There could not be much getting away from that, not that it mattered much. In that case Christopher would be living with his half-sister.﻿ ﻿… Not that it mattered much. It did not matter much, to him, Mark﻿ ﻿… but his father was the sort of man that it might drive to suicide.

A luckless sort of beggar, Christopher!﻿ ﻿… If you took the whole conglobulation at its worst﻿—the father suiciding, the son living with his sister in open sin, the son’s son not his son, and Groby going over to Papist hands.﻿ ﻿… That was the sort of thing that would happen to a Tietjens of the Christopher variety: to any Tietjens who would not get out or get under as he, Mark, had done. Tietjenses took what they damn well got for doing what they damn well wanted to. Well, it landed them in that sort of post.﻿ ﻿… A last post, for, if that boy was not Christopher’s, Groby went out of Tietjens hands. There would be no more Tietjenses. Spelden might well be justified.

The grandfather of Father scalped by Indians in Canada in the war of 1812; the father dying in a place where he should not have been﻿—taking what he got for it and causing quite a scandal for the Court of Victoria; the elder brother of Father killed drunk whilst foxhunting; Father suicided; Christopher a pauper by his own act with a by-blow in his shoes. If then there were to be any more Tietjenses by blood﻿ ﻿… Poor little devils! They would be their own cousins. Something like that.﻿ ﻿…

And possibly none the worse off for that.﻿ ﻿… Either Spelden or Groby Great Tree had perhaps done for the others. Groby Great Tree had been planted to commemorate the birth of Great-grandfather who had died in a whoreshop﻿—and it had always been whispered in Groby, amongst the children and servants, that Groby Great Tree did not like the house. Its roots tore chunks out of the foundations, and two or three times the trunk had had to be bricked into the front wall. It had been brought as a sapling from Sardinia at a time when gentlemen still thought about landscape gardening. A gentleman in those days consulted his heirs about tree planting. Should you plant a group of copper beeches against a group of white maples over against the haha a quarter of a mile from the house so that the contrast seen from the ballroom windows should be agreeable﻿—in thirty years’ time? In those days thought, in families, went in periods of thirty years, owner gravely consulting heir who should see that development of light and shade that the owner never would.

===Chinese===
他到底是不是自杀的？若是，那么瓦伦丁·万诺普便是他的女儿。这一层怕是绕不开的，虽说也没什么大不了。果真如此，克里斯托弗便是同自己同父异母的妹妹同居。……也没什么大不了的。对他、对马克来说，没什么大不了……可他父亲，正是那种可能被逼上自杀一途的男人。

克里斯托弗这个倒霉鬼！……你若把这一整团烂账往最坏处算——父亲自尽，儿子公然同妹妹姘居，儿子的儿子又不是他的儿子，格罗比又落到教皇派手里。……这路事，就偏偏落在克里斯托弗这一号的蒂金斯头上：落在任何一个不肯像他马克这样抽身退步、低头伏小的蒂金斯头上。蒂金斯家的人想干什么就干什么，得了什么也他妈的认什么。得，这就把他们送进了那样一种岗位。……最后一岗。因为，那孩子若不是克里斯托弗的骨血，格罗比就出了蒂金斯家的手。往后再也不会有蒂金斯家的人了。斯佩尔登没准儿真说中了。

父亲的祖父在 1812 年那场战争里、在加拿大让印第安人剥了头皮；父亲死在他本不该去的地方——自作自受，还给维多利亚女王的宫廷惹出一桩大丑闻；父亲的兄长猎狐时醉醺醺地丢了命；父亲自尽了；克里斯托弗亲手把自己折腾成穷光蛋，鞋窠里还揣着个私生儿。倘若这之后血统上当真还会再有几个蒂金斯……可怜的小魔头们！他们得当起自己的表亲。差不多就是这么回事。……

再说，当了自家的表亲，兴许也不吃什么亏。……前头那几代人，不是斯佩尔登咒的、就是格罗比大树妨的，也未可知。格罗比大树原是为庆贺曾祖父降生而栽——那位曾祖父后来死在窑子里——而格罗比地面上，仆人和孩子之间素来悄悄传说，格罗比大树不喜欢这宅子。它的根把房基撕下一块块，有两三回，树身只好砌进前墙里去。它还是一株树苗的时候，从撒丁岛（Sardinia）运来，那还是绅士们肯在风景造园上用心的年代。那些日子，绅士种树要同继承人商量。离宅子四分之一英里、正对隐垣（haha，暗设的园界矮墙）的地方，你该不该让一丛紫铜色山毛榉衬着一丛白枫，好叫三十年之后从舞厅的窗子里望出去，那对比瞧着熨帖？那些年头，人家的盘算是以三十年为期的：业主郑重其事地征询继承人——那片明暗将来长成什么样子，见得着的正是继承人，业主自己是永远见不着的。

===Original===
Nowadays the heir apparently consulted the owner as to whether the tenant who was taking the ancestral home furnished might not cut down trees in order to suit the sanitary ideas of the day.﻿ ﻿… An American day! Well, why not? Those people could not be expected to know how picturesque a contrast the tree would make against the roofs of Groby Great House when seen from Peel’s Moorside. They would never hear of Peel’s Moorside, or John Peel, or the coat so grey.﻿ ﻿…

Apparently that was the meaning of the visit of that young colt and Mrs. de Bray Pape. They had come to ask his, Mark’s, sanction as owner to cut down Groby Great Tree. And then they had funked it and bolted. At any rate the boy was still talking earnestly to the woman in white over the hedge. As to where Mrs. de Bray Pape was, he had no means of knowing; she might be among the potato rows studying the potatoes of the poor for all he knew. He hoped she would not come upon Marie Léonie, because Marie Léonie would make short work of Mrs. de Bray Pape and be annoyed on top of it.

But they were wrong to funk talking to *him* about cutting down Groby Great Tree. He cared nothing about it. Mrs. de Bray Pape might just as well have come and said cheerfully: “Hullo, old cock, we’re going to cut down your bally old tree and let some light into the house.﻿ ﻿…” if that was the way Americans talked when they were cheerful; he had no means of knowing. He never remembered to have talked to an American.﻿ ﻿… Oh, yes, to Cammy Fittleworth! She had certainly been a dreadfully slangy young woman before her husband came into the title. But then Fittleworth was confoundedly slangy too. They said he had to give up in the middle of a speech he tried to make in the House of Lords because he could not do without the word “toppin,” which upset the Lord Chancellor.﻿ ﻿… So there was no knowing what Mrs. de Bray Pape might not have said if she had not thought she was addressing a syphilitic member of an effete aristocracy mad about an old cedar tree. But she might just as well have cheerfully announced it. He did not care. Groby Great Tree had never seemed to like him. It never seemed to like anybody. They say it never forgave the Tietjenses for transplanting it from nice warm Sardinia to that lugubrious climate.﻿ ﻿… That was what the servants said to the children and the children whispered to each other in the dark corridors.

But poor old Christopher! He was going to go mad if the suggestion were made to him. The barest hint! Poor old Christopher, who was now probably at that very minute in one of those beastly machines overhead, coming back from Groby.﻿ ﻿… If Christopher *had* to buy a beastly South Country show-cottage, Mark wished he would not have bought it so near a confounded air-station. However, he expected, probably, that beastly Americans would come flying in the beastly machines to buy the beastly old junk. They did indeed do so﻿—sent by Mr. Schatzweiler, who was certainly efficient except in the sending of cheques.

===Chinese===
如今呢，显然轮到继承人征询业主：那位连家具一并租下祖宅的租客，可不可以为了合乎时下的卫生观念砍几棵树。……一个美国人的世道！唉，有什么不可？总不能指望那些人懂得，从皮尔的荒山坡（Peel's Moorside）上望去，大树衬着格罗比大宅的屋顶是怎样如画的一笔。他们永远不会听说皮尔的荒山坡，不会听说约翰·皮尔（John Peel），也不会听说那件灰外套。……

照理说，那个愣小子和德布雷·佩普夫人那天登门，为的就是这个。他们原是来求他——求马克——以业主的身份点头，好砍掉格罗比大树。后来又怯了场，溜了。反正那小子这会儿还在树篱那头，跟那位白衣太太恳谈。至于德布雷·佩普夫人眼下在哪儿，他无从知晓；没准儿正钻在土豆垄里，考察穷人的土豆，天知道。他只盼她别撞上玛丽·莱昂妮，因为玛丽·莱昂妮会三下五除二把德布雷·佩普夫人打发掉，末了自己还要生上一场气。

可他们怯于同*他*谈砍格罗比大树，实在是多虑了。他才不在乎呢。德布雷·佩普夫人满可以欢欢喜喜进来说：「喂，老家伙，我们要来砍你那棵破老树啦，好叫屋里透点儿光。……」——倘若美国人高兴起来是这么个说话路数的话；他无从知道。他不记得这辈子同哪个美国人说过话。……哦，有的，同卡米·菲特尔沃思（Cammy Fittleworth）说过！她丈夫承袭爵位之前，她可是个满口俚语、轻狂得吓人的姑娘。不过菲特尔沃思本人也是一口了不得的俚语。人家说，他想在上议院演说，说到一半只得撂下，因为他离了「托平」（toppin，俚语：顶好）这个词就开不了口，闹得大法官阁下老大不自在。……所以说，德布雷·佩普夫人若不是把马克当成了没落贵族里一个抱着老雪松发疯的梅毒鬼，谁知道她那张嘴还有什么话说不出来。可她也满可以痛痛快快直说的。他不在乎。格罗比大树似乎从来不曾喜欢过他。它似乎也从来不曾喜欢过谁。人家说，蒂金斯家把它从温暖可意的撒丁岛移栽到那阴惨惨的气候里，它始终没肯饶恕。……这是仆人们讲给孩子听的，孩子们又在黑黢黢的走廊里咬耳朵。

可是可怜的老克里斯托弗！这话只要有人向他提上半句，他就要疯的。半句口风都不行！可怜的老克里斯托弗，这会儿多半正坐在头顶上那种讨厌的机器里，从格罗比往回飞。……克里斯托弗要是非买一座南边的花架子农舍不可，马克只盼他别买得离一座该死的航空站这么近。不过他料想，多半正指着那些讨厌的美国人坐着讨厌的机器飞来，买那些讨厌的破旧货呢。他们也确实来——都是沙茨韦勒先生遣来的，那位先生办事确实麻利，只是寄支票一项除外。

===Original===
Christopher had nearly jumped out of his skin﻿—that is to say, he had sat as still as a lump of white marble﻿—when he had gathered that Sylvia and, still more his own heir, wanted to let Groby furnished. He had said to Mark, over Sylvia’s first letter: “You won’t let ’em?” and Mark knew the agony that was behind his tallowy mask and goggle eyes.﻿ ﻿… Perfectly white around the nostrils he went﻿—that was the sign!

And it had been as near to an appeal as he had ever come﻿—unless the request for a loan on Armistice Day could be regarded as an appeal. But Mark did not think that that could be regarded as a score. In their game neither of them had yet made a real score. Probably neither of them ever would; they were a stout pair of North Countrymen whatever else could be said against them.

No: it hadn’t been a score when Christopher had said: “You won’t let ’em let Groby?” the day before yesterday: Christopher had been in an agony, but he was not *asking* Mark not to let Groby be let; he was only seeking information as to how far Mark would let the degradation of the old place go. Mark had let him pretty well know that Groby might be pulled down and replaced by a terra-cotta hotel before he would stir a finger. On the other hand, Christopher had only to stir a finger and not a blade of grass between the cobbles in the Stillroom Yard could be grubbed up.﻿ ﻿… But by the rules of the game neither of them could give an order. Neither. Mark said to Christopher: “Groby’s yours!” Christopher said to Mark: “Groby’s yours!” With perfect good-humour and coldness. So probably the old place would fall to pieces or Sylvia would turn it into a bawdyhouse.﻿ ﻿… It was a good joke! A good, grim Yorkshire joke!

===Chinese===
克里斯托弗一听说西尔维娅——尤其是他自己的继承人——想把格罗比连家具租出去，差点儿没跳起来——所谓差点儿跳起来，就是他坐得像一尊白大理石像般纹丝不动。西尔维娅头一封信来的时候，他对马克说：「你不会容他们吧？」马克知道，那副猪油色的面具和鼓眼睛背后，藏着的是怎样一场煎熬。……他鼻孔四周刷地一下白了——这就是凭证！

那是他生平离哀求最近的一次——除非停战日那天开口借钱也算得一场哀求。可马克不认为那算得上一次得分。他们这场游戏里，两个人谁也还没真正得过一分。多半谁也永远不会得；且不说别的短处，他们总归是一对结结实实的北方汉子。

不：前天克里斯托弗说「你不会容他们把格罗比租出去吧？」的时候，也算不得一次得分：克里斯托弗当时正熬着苦刑，可他并不是在*求*马克别把格罗比租出去；他不过是打探打探，这老宅子的败坏，马克打算容它走到哪一步。马克思明明白白让他知道了：格罗比就是拆平了改建一座陶砖旅馆，他也不会动一根指头。反过来，克里斯托弗只要动一根指头，蒸馏房院子（Stillroom Yard）里鹅卵石缝间的一根草也休想给人刨掉。……只是按这场游戏的规矩，他们两个谁也不能下命令。谁也不能。马克对克里斯托弗说：「格罗比是你的！」克里斯托弗对马克说：「格罗比是你的！」带着十足的好脾气和十足的冷淡。于是多半这老宅子要么散架坍塌，要么让西尔维娅开成妓馆。……这笑话不赖！一个不赖的、冷峭的约克郡笑话！

===Original===
It was impossible to know which of them suffered more. Christopher, it is true, was having his heart broken because the house suffered﻿—but, damn it, wasn’t Mark himself pretty well heartbroken because Christopher refused to accept the house from him?﻿ ﻿… It was impossible to know which!

Yes, his confounded heart had been broken on Armistice Day in the morning﻿—between the morning and the morning after.﻿ ﻿… Yes: after Christopher had been reading Boswell aloud, night after night for three weeks.﻿ ﻿… Was that playing the game? Was it playing the game to get no sleep if you had not forgiven your brother.﻿ ﻿… Oh, no doubt it was playing the game. You don’t forgive your brother if he lets you down in a damn beastly way.﻿ ﻿… And of course it *is* letting a fellow down in a beastly﻿—a beastly!﻿—way to let him know that you believe he lives on the immoral earnings of his wife.﻿ ﻿… Mark had done that to Christopher. It was unforgivable all right. And equally of course you do not hurt your brother except on the lines circumscribed by the nature of the offence: you are the best friend he has﻿—except on the lines circumscribed by the offence; and he will nurse you like a blasted soft woman﻿—except in so far as the lines circumscribed by the offence do not preclude your ministrations.

For, obviously, the best thing Christopher could have done for his brother’s health would have been to have accepted the stewardship of Groby﻿—but his brother could die and he himself could die before he would do that. It was nevertheless a pretty cruel affair.﻿ ﻿… Over Boswell the two brothers had got as thick as thieves with an astonishing intimacy﻿—and with an astonishing similarity. If one of them made a comment on Bennett Langton it would be precisely the comment that the other had on his lips. It was what asses call telepathy nowadays﻿ ﻿… a warm, comfortable feeling, late at night with the light shaded from your eyes, the voice going on through the deep silence of London that awaited the crashes of falling bombs.﻿ ﻿… Well, Mark accepted Christopher’s dictum that he himself was an eighteenth-century bloke and was only forestalled when he had wanted to tell Christopher that he was more old-fashioned still﻿—a sort of seventeenth-century Anglican who ought to be strolling in a grove with the Greek Testament beneath the arm and all.﻿ ﻿… And, hang it all, there was room for him! The land had not changed.﻿ ﻿… There were still the deep beech-woods making groves beside the ploughlands and the rooks rising lazily as the plough came towards them. The land had not changed.﻿ ﻿… Well, the breed had not changed.﻿ ﻿… There was Christopher.﻿ ﻿… Only, the times﻿ ﻿… they had changed.﻿ ﻿… The rooks and the ploughlands and the beeches and Christopher were there still.﻿ ﻿… But not the frame of mind in the day.﻿ ﻿… The sun might rise and go above the plough till it set behind the hedge, and the ploughman went off to the inn settle; and the moon could do the same. But they would﻿—neither sun nor moon﻿—look on the spit of Christopher in all their journeys. Never. They might as well expect to see a mastodon.﻿ ﻿… And he, Mark, himself was an old-fashioned buffer. That was all right. Judas Iscariot himself was an old-fashioned ass, once upon a time!

===Chinese===
没法知道他们俩哪一个更受熬煎。不错，克里斯托弗的心是替这宅子碎的——可是，妈的，克里斯托弗死活不肯从他手里接下这宅子，马克自己的心难道不也碎得差不多了？……没法知道是哪一个！

是的，他那颗该死的心，是在停战日的早晨碎的——从那天早晨到第二天早晨之间。……不错：是在克里斯托弗夜复一夜朗读了三个星期鲍斯威尔之后。……这算守规矩吗？没饶恕兄弟，就夜夜不叫自己合眼，这算守规矩吗。……哦，多半算是守规矩的。兄弟以一种他妈的歹毒方式撂了你的台，你自然不饶他。……当然，叫一个人知道你认定他吃的是老婆的皮肉钱，这也确实是——确实是！——一种歹毒之至的撂台方式。马克对克里斯托弗干的就是这个。着实不可饶恕。同样当然的是：伤兄弟，也只能伤在罪过性质划下的界线之内；你是他最好的朋友——只在罪过划下的界线之内是；他会像个该死的软心女人那样看护你——只要罪过划下的界线不曾把你该得的照料也一并划掉。

因为，明摆着，为了弟弟的身子骨，克里斯托弗能做的最好的事，本是接下格罗比的代管——可他弟弟尽可以死，他自个儿也尽可以死，他才不肯那么办。尽管如此，这仍是一桩相当狠心的事。……鲍斯威尔叫这兄弟俩好得如胶似漆，亲得叫人吃惊——像得也叫人吃惊。一个对本尼特·兰顿（Bennett Langton）刚下一句考语，那准是另一个嘴边正要出口的一句。这就是如今蠢货们所谓的「心灵感应」……一种暖融融、舒舒服服的感觉：夜深了，灯光罩着不晃眼，那嗓音穿过伦敦深浓的静默一路念下去——那座城正候着炸弹坠落的轰响。……唉，克里斯托弗断言他马克本人是个十八世纪的老古董，马克认了这笔账；而他原也想告诉克里斯托弗，他比自己想的还要老派——简直是十七世纪的国教徒一名，本该腋下夹一册希腊文《新约》，在小树林里踱步来着——只是话没出口，先叫他抢了先。……再说，见他的鬼，这世上原也容得下他！这片土地没有变。……耕地旁边，深深的山毛榉林子照样结成树丛；犁头走近了，秃鼻乌鸦才懒洋洋地飞起。这片土地没有变。……唉，这个人种也没有变。……克里斯托弗不就在那儿。……只是，时世……时世变了。……乌鸦、耕地、山毛榉、克里斯托弗，都还在。……可这世道里的那副心境，没了。……太阳尽管升起来，走在犁沟上头，直到落进树篱背后，犁田人尽管收工上酒馆的高背长凳去；月亮也尽可以照样再来一遍。可它们——太阳也罢，月亮也罢——周天巡行到哪一遭，也照不见克里斯托弗那个模子里倒出来的人了。永远照不见。要照见，还不如指望见着一头乳齿象。……而他马克本人，也是个老派的老古董。这没什么不好。想当年，加略人犹大不也曾是个老派的蠢货么！

===Original===
But it was almost on the edge of not playing the game for Christopher to let that intimacy establish itself and all the time to cherish that unforgivingness.﻿ ﻿… Not quite not playing the game: but almost. For hadn’t Mark held out feelers? Hadn’t he made concessions? Hadn’t his very marrying of Marie Léonie been by way of a concession to Christopher? Didn’t Christopher, if the truth was to be known, want Mark to marry Marie Léonie because he, Christopher, wanted to marry Valentine Wannop and hadn’t a hope? If the truth were known.﻿ ﻿… Well, he had made that concession to Christopher, who was a sort of a parson anyhow. But ought Christopher to have exacted﻿—to have telepathically willed﻿—that concession if he wasn’t himself going to concede something? Ought he to have forced him, Mark, to accept his mooning womanly services when the poor devil was already worn out with his military duties of seeing old tins cleaned out day after day, if he meant to become a beastly old-furniture dealer and refuse Groby? For, upon his soul, till the morning of Armistice Day, Mark had accepted Christopher’s story of Mr. Schatzweiler as merely a good-humoured, grim threat.﻿ ﻿… A sort of a feint at a threat.﻿ ﻿…

Well, probably it was playing the game all right: if Christopher thought it was jonnock, jonnock it was!

But﻿ ﻿… a damn beastly shock.﻿ ﻿… Why, he had been practically convalescent, he had been out of bed in a dressing-gown and had told Lord Wolstonmark that he could pile in as many papers as he liked from the Office.﻿ ﻿… And then Christopher, without a hat and in a beastly civilian suit of light mulberry-coloured Harris tweed, had burst into the room with a beastly piece of old furniture under his arm.﻿ ﻿… A sort of inlaid toy writing-desk. A model. For cabinetmakers! A fine thing to bring into a convalescent bedroom, to a man quietly reading Form T.O. LOUWR 1962 E 17 of the 10/11/18, in front of a clean fire.﻿ ﻿… And chalk-white about the gills the fellow was﻿—with an awful lot of silver in his hair.﻿ ﻿… What age was he? Forty? Forty-three? God knew!

===Chinese===
可克里斯托弗一面由着那份亲密扎下根来，一面又始终揣着那份不饶恕，这就差不多要擦着「不守规矩」的边儿了。……还不算真的不守规矩：可是差不多了。难道马克没有伸出过触须？难道他没有让过步？他娶玛丽·莱昂妮这一桩，本身难道不就是对克里斯托弗的一种让步？说穿了，克里斯托弗自己想娶瓦伦丁·万诺普而又没指望，他岂不正巴望马克娶了玛丽·莱昂妮？说穿了就是这样。……唉，这个让步他是让给克里斯托弗了——那人横竖算得上半个牧师。可克里斯托弗自己若不打算也让出点什么，他该不该逼着——该不该用心灵感应逼着——要这一让步？他既然打定主意做个讨厌的旧家具贩子、回掉格罗比，那还该不该硬逼他马克——这个可怜虫已被日复一日监刷旧铁罐的军务磨得油尽灯枯——去受用他那套痴痴的女性服侍？因为，凭灵魂起誓，直到停战日那天早晨，马克一直只把克里斯托弗那套沙茨韦勒先生的说辞，当作一句好脾气的、冷冷的威吓。……一种虚晃一枪的威吓。……

唉，多半还是守规矩的：克里斯托弗既然觉得这算「乔诺克」（jonnock，北方土话：公道、地道），那它就是乔诺克！

可是……好一个他妈的歹毒的打击。……想想看，他当时简直已经在康复期了，已经披着晨衣下了床，还告诉了沃尔斯顿马克勋爵：从部里往他这儿搬文件，搬多少都随他的便。……跟着克里斯托弗就闯进来了：帽子也不戴，穿一身浅桑葚色哈里斯花呢（Harris tweed）的讨厌便服，胳膊底下夹着一件讨厌的旧家具。……一种镶嵌的小玩意儿写字台。一个模型。给柜匠看的模型！拿到康复期病人的卧室里来，端给一个坐在旺旺的净火前、安安静静读着 10/11/18 号表格 T.O. LOUWR 1962 E 17 的人——真是件妙事。……那家伙腮帮子灰白灰白的——头发里夹着好一把好一把的银丝。……他多大岁数了？四十？四十三？天知道！

===Original===
Forty.﻿ ﻿… He wanted to borrow forty quid on that beastly piece of furniture. To have an Armistice Day Beanfeast and set up house with his gal! Forty quid! My God! Mark felt his bowels turning over within him with disgust.﻿ ﻿… The gal﻿—that fellow’s half-sister as like as not﻿—was waiting in an empty house for him to go and seduce her. In order to celebrate the salvation of the world by seven million deaths!

If you seduce a girl you don’t do it on forty pounds: you accept Groby and three, seven, ten thousand a year. So he had told Christopher.

And then he had got it. Full in the face. Christopher was not going to accept a penny from him. Never. Not ever!﻿ ﻿… No doubt about that, either. That fact had gone into Mark as a knife goes into the stag’s throat. It had hurt as much, but it hadn’t killed! Damn it, it might as well have! It might as well have.﻿ ﻿… Does a fellow do that to his own brother just because his own brother has called him﻿ ﻿… what is the word? *Maquereau!*﻿ ﻿… Probably a *maquereau* is worse than a pimp.﻿ ﻿… The difference between a flea and a louse, as Dr. Johnson said.

===Chinese===
四十上下。……他想拿那件讨厌的家具押借四十镑。好办一场停战日的吃喝庆祝，同他的姑娘成家过日子！四十镑！我的老天！马克只觉得五脏六腑在肚肠里翻腾，一阵厌恶。……那姑娘——十有八九是那家伙同父异母的妹妹——正在一座空宅子里，等他去施行诱奸。为的是庆贺这个世界凭七百万条人命换来的得救！

你要诱奸一个姑娘，总不能只凭四十镑：要凭，你就得接受格罗比，外加一年三千、七千、一万镑。他是这么对克里斯托弗说的。

跟着他就挨上了那一下。正正地打在脸面上。克里斯托弗不打算从他手里受一个便士。永远不。这辈子都不！……这一点也没得怀疑。那个事实扎进马克的身体，就像刀子扎进公鹿的喉咙。一样地疼，可它没宰了他！妈的，倒不如宰了的好！倒不如宰了的好。……一个人对亲兄弟下这样的手，就因为亲兄弟叫过他……那词儿是什么来着？*Maquereau*（法语：龟公）！……龟公没准儿比皮条客还难听。……正如约翰逊博士所言，是跳蚤和虱子的分别。

===Original===
Eh, but Christopher was bitter!﻿ ﻿… Apparently he had gone round first to Sir John Robertson’s with the jigamaree. Years before, Sir John had promised to buy it for a hundred pounds. It was a special sort of model signed by some duke of a Bath cabinetmaker in 1762.﻿ ﻿… Wasn’t that the year of the American Rebellion? Well, Christopher had bought it in a junk-shop of sorts for a fiver and Sir John had promised him a hundred quid. He collected cabinetmakers’ models: extraordinarily valuable they were. Christopher had spat out that this was worth a thousand dollars.﻿ ﻿… Thinking of his old-furniture customers!

When Christopher had used that word﻿—with the blue pebbles sticking out of his white-lard head﻿—Mark had felt the sweat break out all over him. He had known it was all up.﻿ ﻿… Christopher had gone on: you expected him to spit electric sparks but his voice was wooden. Sir John had said to him:

“Eh, no, mon. You’re a fine soldier now, raping half the girls in Flanders and Ealing and asking us to regard you as heroes. Fine heroes. And now you’re safe.﻿ ﻿… A hundred pounds is a price to a Christian that is faithful to his lovely wife. Five pounds is as much as I’ll give you for the model, and be thankful it is five, not one, for old sake’s sake!”

That was what Sir John Robertson had said to Christopher: that was what the world was like to serving soldiers in that day. You don’t have to wonder that Christopher was bitter﻿—even to his own brother with the sweat making his under-linen icy. Mark had said:

===Chinese===
唉，克里斯托弗这一肚子苦水！……看样子，他先前先带着那件破玩意儿跑了一趟约翰·罗伯逊爵士家。好些年前，约翰爵士曾许诺出一百镑买下它。那是个特别的模型，1762 年由巴斯一位柜匠巨擘签名。……那不是美国叛乱那一年么？总归，克里斯托弗是在一家旧货破烂铺子里花五镑淘来的，约翰爵士许了他一百镑。他专收柜匠模型：那东西金贵得很。克里斯托弗一口啐出来：这玩意儿值一千美元。……心里想的是他的旧家具主顾们！

克里斯托弗说出那个词的时候——蓝色鹅卵石般的眼睛从他那颗白猪油的脑袋上暴凸出来——马克只觉得浑身上下一下子爆出一层汗。他知道全完了。……克里斯托弗还接着往下讲：你只当他嘴里要吐电火花，他的声音却木头似的。约翰爵士对他说：

「咄，不成的，伙计。你如今是个好兵爷了，把法兰德斯和伊灵的姑娘糟蹋了一半，还指望我们拿你们当英雄。好个英雄。如今你太平了。……一百镑，是给那位对可爱妻子忠贞不二的基督徒的价钱。这模型我至多给你五镑；看在老交情份上，是五镑不是一镑，你就该谢天谢地啰！」

这就是约翰·罗伯逊爵士对克里斯托弗说的话：那个年头，世道对待现役军人就是这副嘴脸。克里斯托弗满肚子苦水，你不必奇怪——连对他亲兄弟也是那副苦相，冷汗把他的贴身内衣浸得冰凉。马克说：

===Original===
“My good chap. I won’t lend you a penny on that idiotic jigamaree. But I’ll write you a cheque for a thousand pounds this minute. Give me my chequebook from the table.﻿ ﻿…”

Marie Léonie had come into the room on hearing Christopher’s voice. She liked to hear the news from Christopher. And she liked Christopher and Mark to have heated discussions. She had observed that they did Mark good: on the day when Christopher had first come there, three weeks before, when they certainly had heatedly discussed she had observed that Mark’s temperature had fallen from ninety-nine point six to ninety-eight point two. In two hours.﻿ ﻿… After all, if a Yorkshire man can quarrel he can live. They were like that, those others, she said.

Christopher had turned on her and said:

===Chinese===
「好伙计。那个蠢玩意儿，我一个便士也不押给你。可我这就给你开一张一千镑的支票。把我的支票本从桌上递给我。……」

玛丽·莱昂妮听见克里斯托弗的声音，进了屋来。她爱听克里斯托弗带来的新闻。她也爱看克里斯托弗和马克热火朝天地争论。她看出这对他马克有好处：三周前克里斯托弗头一回来的那天，他们确实大吵了一场，她观察到马克的体温从 99.6 度降到了 98.2 度。两个钟头之内。……说到底，约克郡人只要还吵得动架，就死不了。他们那些人就是这个样子，她说。

克里斯托弗转过身来，冲着她说：

===Original===
“*Ma belle amie m’attend à ma maison; nous voulons célébrer avec mes camarades de regiment. Je n’ai pas le sous. Prêtez moi quarante livres, je vous en prie, madame!*” He had added that he would leave his cabinet as a pledge. He was as stiff as a sentry outside Buckingham Palace. She had looked at Mark with some astonishment. After all, she might well be astonished. He himself had made no sign and suddenly Christopher had exclaimed:

“*Prêtez les moi, prêtez les moi, pour l’amour de Dieu!*”

Marie Léonie had gone a little white, but she had turned up her skirt and turned down her stocking and took out the notes.

“*Pour le dieu d’Amour, monsieur, je veux bien*,” she had said.﻿ ﻿… You never knew what a Frenchwoman would not say. That was out of an old song.

===Chinese===
「*Ma belle amie m'attend à ma maison; nous voulons célébrer avec mes camarades de regiment. Je n'ai pas le sous. Prêtez moi quarante livres, je vous en prie, madame!*」（我的美人儿在家里等着我；我要同团里的弟兄们庆贺一番。我身无分文。请借我四十镑，夫人！）他又补了一句，说愿把那口柜子留下作押。他僵直得像白金汉宫门外的卫兵。她带着几分惊诧望了望马克。说到底，她也难怪惊诧。他自己半点没吱声，克里斯托弗忽然就嚷了起来：

「*Prêtez les moi, prêtez les moi, pour l'amour de Dieu!*」（借给我吧，借给我吧，看在上帝份上！）

玛丽·莱昂妮脸上微微发白，可她掀起裙角，翻下长袜，掏出了钞票。

「*Pour le dieu d'Amour, monsieur, je veux bien*（看在爱神的份上，先生，我愿意），」她说。……你永远料不到一个法国女人什么样的话说不出口。那是老歌里的一句词儿。

---

===Original===
But the sweat burst out all over his face at the recollection: great drops of sweat.

===Chinese===
可一想起这些，汗水便涌满他整张脸：大颗大颗的汗珠。

### VII / 第七章

===Original===
Marie Léonie, a strong taste of apples in her mouth, strong savours of apples on the air, wasps around her and as if a snowdrift of down descending about her feet, was frowning seriously over Burgundy bottles into which ran cider from a glass tube that she held to their necks. She frowned because the task was serious and engrossing, because the wasps annoyed her and because she was resisting an impulse inside herself. It told her that something ailed Mark and urged her to go and look at him.

It annoyed her because, as a rule﻿—a rule so strong that it had assumed the aspect of a regulation﻿—she felt presages of something ailing Mark only at night. Only at night. During the day usually she felt in her *for intérieur* that Mark was like what he was only because he wanted so to be. His glance was too virile and dominant to let you think otherwise﻿—the dark, liquid, direct glance! But at nightfall﻿—or at any rate shortly after supper when she had retired to her room terrible premonitions of disaster to Mark visited her. He was dying where he lay; he was beset by the spectral beings of the countryside; robbers, even, had crept upon him, though that was unreasonable. For all the countryside knew that Mark was paralysed and unable to store wealth in his mattress.﻿ ﻿… Still, nefarious strangers might see him and imagine that he kept his gold repeater watch beneath his pillow.﻿ ﻿… So she would rise a hundred times in a night and, going to the low, diamond-casement window, would lean out and listen. But there would be no sound: the wind in the leaves; the cry of waterbirds over head. The dim light would be in the hut, seen unmoving through the apple-boughs.

Now, however, in broad daylight, towards the hour of tea, with the little maid on a stool beside her plucking the boiling-hens that were to go to market next day, with the boxes of eggs on their shelves, each egg wired to the bottom of its box, waiting till she had time to date-stamp it﻿—in the open potting-shed in the quiet, broad light of a summer day she was visited by a presage of something ailing Mark. She resented it, but she was not the woman to resist it.
===Chinese===
玛丽·莱昂妮嘴里一股浓烈的苹果味，空气里也是浓烈的苹果香，黄蜂围着她嗡嗡打转，拔下的绒毛像小雪堆似的纷纷扬扬落向她脚边——她正对着几只勃艮第酒瓶蹙眉凝神，手里握着一根玻璃管抵在瓶颈上，苹果酒正顺着管子流进瓶里。她蹙眉，因为这活计要认真专注，因为黄蜂搅得她心烦，也因为她正抵御着心头的一股冲动。那冲动告诉她：马克有点不对头，催她过去看看。

这叫她恼火，因为照例——这条例子硬得已经近乎一条规矩——她只在夜里才感应得到马克有什么不对头的预兆。只在夜里。白天，她通常在自己的内心深处（*for intérieur*）觉得，马克之所以是眼下这副样子，只因为他自己愿意如此。他那一瞥太雄强、太有威势，容不得你多想——那乌黑、莹润、直直的一瞥！可一到天黑——或者无论如何，晚饭后不久、她退回自己房里的时候，马克大祸临头的可怕预感便来造访她。他正躺在他躺着的地方咽气；他被乡野的阴魂野鬼团团围住；甚至有强盗摸到了他跟前，虽然这于理不通。因为全乡的人都知道马克瘫了，不会把钱财藏进褥子底下。……不过，歹毒的生人撞见他，兴许会猜想他那块金质打簧怀表就藏在枕头底下。……于是她一夜要起身百来次，走到那扇低矮的菱格铅条窗前，探身出去谛听。可四下里没有声息：只有风声过叶，水禽的啼叫掠过头顶。茅棚里那盏昏黄的灯，透过苹果树枝桠望去，一动不动。

然而此刻，光天化日之下，快到下午茶的时辰，小女仆坐在她身旁的凳子上，给第二天要送去集市的炖汤老母鸡拔毛；一盒盒鸡蛋搁在架上，每只蛋都用细铁丝拴牢在盒底，只等她腾出手来打上日期戳——就在敞亮的育苗棚里，在夏日恬静的漫天白光里，马克有什么不对头的预感竟又来造访她。她恼这预感，可她不是能抗拒它的那种女人。

===Original===
There was, however, nothing to warrant it. From the corner of the house to which she proceeded she could see quite well the greater part of Mark’s solitary figure. Gunning, being talked to by the English lord, held a spare horse by the bridle and was looking at Mark over the hedge. He exhibited no emotions. A young man was walking along the inside of the hedge between it and the raspberries. That was no affair of hers: Gunning was not protesting. The head and shoulders of a young woman﻿—or it might be another young man﻿—were proceeding along the outside of the hedge nearly level with the first one. That was equally no affair of hers. Probably they were looking at the bird’s nest. There was some sort of bird’s nest, she had heard, in that thick hedge. There was no end to the folly of the English in the country as in the town: they would waste time over everything. This bird was a bottle﻿ ﻿… bottle-something, and Christopher and Valentine and the parson and the doctor and the artist who lived down the hill were crazy about it. They walked on tiptoe when they were within twenty yards. Gunning was allowed to trim the hedge, but apparently the birds knew Gunning.﻿ ﻿… For Marie Léonie, all birds were “*moineaux*”; as who should say “sparrers”; in London they called them that﻿—just as all flowers were “*giroflées*”﻿—as you might say wallflowers.﻿ ﻿… No wonder this nation was going to rack and ruin when it wasted its time over preserving the nests of sparrers and naming innumerable wallflowers! The country was well enough﻿—a sort of suburb of Caen: but the people!﻿ ﻿… no wonder William, of Falaise, in Normandy subjugated them with such ease.

Now she had wasted five minutes, for the glass tubes, hinged on rubber, that formed her siphon from barrel to bottle had had perforce to be taken out of the spile-hole; the air had entered into it, and she would have to put it back and suck once more at the tube until the first trickle of cider entered her mouth. She disliked having to do that; it wasted the cider and she disliked the flavour in the afternoon when one had lunched. The little maid also would say: “A-oh, melady-ship, Ah *du* call thet queer!”﻿ ﻿… Nothing would cure that child of saying that though she was otherwise *sage et docile*. Even Gunning scratched his head at the sight of those tubes.

Could these savages never understand that if you want to have *cidre mousseux*﻿—foaming﻿—you must have as little sediment as possible? And that in the bottom of casks, even if they had not been moved for a long time, there will always be sediment﻿—particularly if you set up a flow in the liquid by running it from a tap near the bottom. So you siphon off the top of the great casks for bottling *mousseux*, and drink the rest from the cask, and run the thickest into little thin-wood casks with many hoops for freezing in the winter.﻿ ﻿… To make *calvados* where you cannot have alembics because of the excise.﻿ ﻿… In this unhappy country you may not have alembics for the distilling of applejack, plum-brandy or other *fines*﻿—because of the excise! *Quel pays! Quels gens!*
===Chinese===
然而，并没有什么凭据。她走到宅子的那个墙角，马克孤零零的身影大半都看得分明。冈宁被那位英国勋爵缠着说话，一手拉着缰绳，牵着一匹备用的马，隔着树篱望着马克。他没露出什么情绪。一个年轻男子沿着树篱内侧、在篱笆与覆盆子之间走着。那不关她的事：冈宁没有喝止。一个年轻女人的头和肩膀——也可能是另一个年轻男子——正沿着树篱外侧行进，跟头一个差不多并排。那同样不关她的事。多半他们是在看鸟窝。她听人说过，那道厚树篱里有个什么鸟窝。英国人的荒唐乡下城里都没个完：什么都肯耗工夫。那鸟儿叫瓶儿……瓶儿什么雀来着（bottle-tit，长尾山雀），克里斯托弗、瓦伦丁、牧师、医生，还有住在坡下的那位画家，都为它疯魔。走到二十码之内便踮起脚尖。树篱是准许冈宁修剪的，可那些鸟分明认得冈宁。……在玛丽·莱昂妮那里，天下的鸟统称「麻雀」（*moineaux*）——好比说「家雀儿」；伦敦人管麻雀就叫这个——正如天下的花统称「桂竹香」（*giroflées*）——好比说墙头花。……这个民族连保家雀儿的窝、给数不清的墙头花起名字都肯费工夫，难怪要败落！这乡间倒还不错——像卡昂的郊区：可这的人！……难怪诺曼底法莱斯的威廉降服他们易如反掌。

这一下她已荒废了五分钟，因为那根以橡胶作活动接头的玻璃管——她从桶到瓶的虹吸管——不得不从桶孔里拔了出来；空气钻了进去，她只好重新插上，再用嘴吸管子，直到头一线苹果酒流进嘴里。她不喜欢干这个；糟蹋酒，而且午后吃过午饭，她不喜欢那个味儿。小女仆还会说：「哎哟，夫人您，俺真叫这事儿邪门儿！」……这孩子旁的方面乖顺听话（*sage et docile*），唯独这句话怎么也改不掉。连冈宁见了那些管子都直挠头。

这些野蛮人难道永远不明白：你要是想喝起泡的苹果酒（*cidre mousseux*），酒里的沉淀就得越少越好？还有，桶底哪怕久置不动，也总归有沉淀——尤其你要是开了个近桶底的龙头、让酒流动起来的话。所以，起泡酒要用大桶上层的酒虹吸装瓶，其余的从桶里舀着喝，最稠的那截灌进箍着多道箍的细木小桶，留到冬天上冻。……好在没有蒸馏器的地方——因为货物税嘛——做卡尔瓦多斯（*calvados*，苹果烧酒）。……在这个不幸的国家，蒸馏苹果白兰地、李子白兰地或是别的精馏烧酒（*fines*），都不许你置蒸馏器——就因为货物税！*Quel pays! Quels gens!*（这算什么国家！这算什么国民！）

===Original===
They lacked industry, frugality﻿—and, above all, spirit! Look at that poor Valentine, hiding in her room upstairs because there were people about whom she suspected of being people from the English Lord’s house.﻿ ﻿… By rights that poor Valentine should be helping her with the bottling and ready to sell that lugubrious old furniture to visitors whilst her lord was away buying more old rubbish.﻿ ﻿… And she was distracted because she could not find some prints. They represented﻿—Marie Léonie was well aware because she had heard the facts several times﻿—street criers of ambulant wares in London years ago. There were only eight of these to be found. Where were the other four? The customer, an English lady of title, was anxious for them. As presents for an immediate wedding! Monsieur my brother-in-law had come upon the four that were to make up the set at a sale two days before. He had recounted with satisfaction how he had found them on the grass.﻿ ﻿… It was supposed that he had brought them home; but they were not in the warehouse at Cramp the carpenter’s, they were not to be found left in the cart. They were in no drawer or press.﻿ ﻿… What was to prove that *mon beau-frère* had brought them home from the sale? He was not there: he was gone for a day and a half. Naturally he would be gone for a day and a half when he was most needed.﻿ ﻿… And where was he gone, leaving his young wife in that nervous condition. For a day and a half! He had never before been gone for a day and a half.﻿ ﻿… There was then something brewing; it was in the air; it was in her bones.﻿ ﻿… It was like that dreadful day of the Armistice when this miserable land betrayed the beautiful *pays de France*!﻿ ﻿… When monsieur had borrowed forty pounds of her.﻿ ﻿… In the name of heaven why did not he borrow another forty﻿—or eighty﻿—or a hundred, rather than be distracted and distract Mark and his unhappy girl?﻿ ﻿…

She was not unsympathetic, that girl. She had civilization. She could talk of Philémon and Baucis. She had made her *bachot*, she was what you would call *fille de famille*.﻿ ﻿… But without *chic*.﻿ ﻿… Without﻿ ﻿… Without﻿ ﻿… Well, she neither displayed enough erudition to be a *blas bleu*﻿—though she had enough erudition!﻿—nor enough *chic* to be a *femme légère*﻿—a *poule* who would *faire la noce* with her gallant. Monsieur the brother-in-law was no gay spark. But you never know with a man.﻿ ﻿… The cut of a skirt; a twist of the hair.﻿ ﻿… Though today there was no hair to twist: but there is the equivalent.

And it was a fact that you never knew a man. Look at the case of Eleanor Dupont, who lived for ten years with Duchamp of the Sorbonne.﻿ ﻿… Eleanor would never attend scrupulously to her attire because her man wore blue spectacles and was a *savant*.﻿ ﻿… But what happened.﻿ ﻿… There came along a little piece with a hat as large as cartwheel covered with green-stuff and sleeves up above her ears﻿—as the mode was then.﻿ ﻿…
===Chinese===
他们缺勤勉、缺节俭——尤其缺的是精气神！瞧瞧那个可怜的瓦伦丁，躲在楼上自己房里，只因为宅子附近有她疑心是那位英国勋爵宅上来的人。……按理说，那可怜的瓦伦丁该来帮她装瓶，在她家老爷出门采购更多破烂的时候，随时把那些阴沉沉的旧家具卖给来客。……而她呢，正为找不着几幅版画心神不宁。那几幅画上画的——玛丽·莱昂妮清楚得很，因为那原委她听过好几遍了——是多年前景伦敦街头叫卖杂货的小贩。如今只寻出八幅。另外四幅哪儿去了？买主是位英国贵妇人，正眼巴巴等着呢。要当贺婚礼送人的！小叔先生两天前在一场拍卖会上撞见了凑齐整套的那四幅。他还得意洋洋地讲过自己怎么在草地上捡到它们的。……想来他是把它们带回家了；可克兰普木匠家的仓库里没有，车上也没有遗落。哪个抽屉、哪口柜橱里都没有。……凭什么证明她那位小叔先生（*mon beau-frère*）把它们从拍卖会上带回了家？他人不在：走了一天半。偏偏最用得着他的时候，他准会走它一天半。……他把年轻太太撂在这么个心神不定的境地，自己上哪儿去了？一天半！他以前可从来没有走开过一天半。……这么说来，有什么事在酝酿；它在空气里；在她的骨头缝里。……就跟停战日那可怕的一天一个味道，那天这块悲惨的土地背叛了美丽的法兰西乡土（*pays de France*）！……就在那天，先生向她借走了四十镑。……看在老天的份上，他为什么不再多借四十——或者八十——或者一百，偏偏要自己心烦意乱，还搅得马克和他那不幸的姑娘也不得安生？……

那姑娘倒不是没有可亲之处。她有教养。她谈得来菲利蒙与鲍西丝（Philémon and Baucis）。她考过了中学会考（*bachot*），是那种人称良家闺秀（*fille de famille*）的姑娘。……可就是没有派头（*chic*）。……没有……没有……唉，她的学问露出来，够不上一个女学究（*blas bleu*，蓝袜子）——虽说学问她是够的！——那股派头又够不上一个轻佻女人（*femme légère*）——那种肯陪情郎寻欢作乐（*faire la noce*）的「野鸡」（*poule*）。小叔先生呢，也不是寻欢作乐的轻浮子弟。可男人这种事你永远说不准。……裙子的裁法；鬓发的一绺卷。……虽说如今不兴那种可卷的鬓发了：可总有对等的物事。

男人这种事你永远摸不透，这可是实情。瞧瞧埃莉诺·杜邦的案例，她跟索邦的杜尚同居了十年。……埃莉诺从不肯在衣装上用心，因为她那位先生戴蓝眼镜、是个学问家（*savant*）。……可后来怎么着。……来了个小娘们，帽子大得像车轮，蒙着绿货色，袖子高挑到耳朵以上——那年头正兴这个。……

===Original===
That had been a lesson to her, Marie Léonie, who had been a girl at the time. She had determined that if she achieved a *collage sérieux* with a monsieur of eighty and as blind as a bat she would study the modes of the day right down to the latest perfume. These messieurs did not know it, but they moved among *femmes du monde* and the fashionable cocottes, and however much she at home might be the little brown bird of the domestic hearth, the lines of her dresses, her hair, her personal odour, must conform. Mark did not imagine; she did not suppose he had ever seen a fashionable journal in her apartments that were open to him, or had ever suspected that she walked in the Row on a Sunday when he was away.﻿ ﻿… But she had studied these things like another. And more. For it is difficult to keep with the fashion and at the same time appear as if you were a serious *petite bourgeoise*. But she had done it; and observe the results.﻿ ﻿…

But that poor Valentine.﻿ ﻿… Her man was attached enough: and well he ought to be, considering the affair in which he had landed her. But always there comes the *pic des tempêtes*, the Cap Horn, round which you must go. It is the day when your man looks at you and says: “H’m, h’m,” and considers if the candle is not more valuable than the game! Ah, then.﻿ ﻿… There are wise folk who put that at the seventh year; other wise ones, at the second; others again, at the eleventh.﻿ ﻿… But in fact you may put it at any day on any year﻿—to the hundredth.﻿ ﻿… And that poor Valentine with four spots of oil on her only skirt but two. And that so badly hung, though the stuff no doubt was once good. One must concede that! They make admirable tweeds in this country: better certainly than in Roubaix. But is that enough to save a country﻿—or a woman dependent on a man who has introduced her into a bad affair?

A voice behind her said:
===Chinese===
这对她、对玛丽·莱昂妮是个教训，那时她还年轻。她当时就拿定了主意：哪怕她攀上的是一位八十岁、瞎得像蝙蝠似的老先生，正经结成了终身之好（*collage sérieux*），她也要把时新样式钻研到底，一直钻研到最新的香水。这些先生们自己并不知道，他们周旋于上流交际场的贵妇（*femmes du monde*）与当红名妓之间；她纵然在家做惯了炉灶边的小褐雀，她裙裾的线条、她的头发、她的体香，都必须合乎时式。马克浑然不觉；她料想，在她那套任他进出的公寓里，他从没见过一本时髦刊物，也从没起过疑心，疑心他不在家的星期天她去骑马道（the Row）骑马。……可这些功课她跟旁人一样下了。还下得更深。因为既要跟住时式、又要显得是个正经的小家碧玉（*petite bourgeoise*），这可不容易。可她做到了；你瞧瞧这成果。……

可那可怜的瓦伦丁。……她那个男人黏她黏得也够紧：也该他紧，想想他把她拽进的是怎样一桩事。可是总有一天会驶到风暴角（*pic des tempêtes*），就是合恩角（Cap Horn），这道角你是绕不过去的。就是那一天，你的男人瞧着你，咳一声：「唔，唔，」掂量起这局牌还值不值那支蜡烛！啊，到了那时。……有聪明人把这日子定在第七年；另有聪明人定在第二年；还有定在第十一年的。……可事实上你把它定在哪一年哪一天都行——定到第一百年也使得。……而那可怜的瓦伦丁，她仅有的两条裙子上还沾着四点油渍。裁剪得又那么蹩脚，虽说料子想必原先是好的。这可得认账！这个国家的花呢做得确实出色：比鲁贝（Roubaix）的强，这是肯定的。可这够救一个国家吗——够救一个委身于男人、而这男人又把她拖进了坏事的女人吗？

她背后有个声音说：

===Original===
“I see you have plenty of eggs!”﻿—an unusual voice of a sort of breathless nervousness. Marie Léonie continued to hold the mouth of her tube into the neck of a burgundy bottle; into this she had already introduced a small screw of sifted sugar and an extremely minute portion of a powder that she got from a pharmacist of Rouen. This, she understood, made the cider of a rich brownness. She did not see why cider should be brown, but it was considered to be less fortifying if it were light golden. She continued also to think about Valentine, who would be twittering with nerves at the window whose iron-leaded casement was open above their heads. She would have put down her Latin book and have crept to the window to listen.

The little girl beside Marie Léonie had risen from the three-legged stool and held a dead, white fowl with a nearly naked breast by its neck. She said hoarsely:

“These ’ere be ’er Ladyship’s settins of prize Reds.” She was blonde, red-faced and wore on her dull fair hair a rather large cap, on her thin body a check blue cotton gown. “Arf a crownd a piece the heggs be or twenty-four shillings a dozen if you takes a gross.”
===Chinese===
「我瞧您这儿的鸡蛋可真不少！」——一副透不过气来的紧张劲儿的不寻常嗓音。玛丽·莱昂妮兀自把管口抵在一只勃艮第酒瓶的瓶颈里；瓶里她早已放入一小卷筛过的砂糖，还有针尖大的一点儿粉末，那是从鲁昂一位药剂师手里弄来的。据她所知，这能让苹果酒泛出浓浓的棕褐色。她不明白苹果酒为什么要棕的，可人们认定，淡金色的补力不够。她脑子里也还在想瓦伦丁，这会儿准在头顶上那扇开着铅条格窗的窗口，紧张得像小雀儿似的吱吱喳喳。她该是搁下了那本拉丁文的书，蹑手蹑脚挪到窗口去听了。

玛丽·莱昂妮身边的小姑娘从三脚凳上站了起来，拎着一只胸脯褪得几乎精光的白花花死母鸡的脖子。她哑着嗓子说：

「这些个是『那位夫人』（’Er Ladyship）的名种红鸡下的蛋。」她金发红脸，暗淡的黄头发上戴着一顶挺大的软帽，瘦身上穿着蓝格子棉布裙。「蛋是两先令六便士一个，您要整拿一罗（gross），一打就算二十四先令。」

===Original===
Marie Léonie heard the hoarse voice with some satisfaction. This girl whom they had only had for a fortnight seemed to be satisfactory mentally; it was not her business to sell the eggs but Gunning’s; nevertheless she knew the details. She did not turn round: it was not *her* business to talk to anyone who wanted to buy eggs and she had no curiosity as to customers. She had too much else to think about. The voice said:

“Half a crown seems a great deal for an egg. What is that in dollars? This must be that tyranny over edibles by the producer of which one has heard so much.”

“Tiddn nothin’ in dollars,” the girl said. “Arf a dollar is two bob. Arf a crownd is two ’n’ six.”
===Chinese===
听见这副哑嗓门，玛丽·莱昂妮有几分满意。这姑娘他们才用了两个礼拜，脑子似乎还使得；卖鸡蛋本不是她的事，是冈宁的事；可内中细节她门儿清。她没有回头：跟买鸡蛋的主顾搭话不是「她」的事，她对主顾也毫无好奇。她要想的事太多了。那声音说：

「半克朗买一个蛋，好像贵得可以。这折成美元是多少？这想必就是人们常听说的、生产者对吃食的专政吧。」

「折美元算不了啥，」小姑娘说。「半个美元就是两先令。半克朗就是两先令六。」

===Original===
The conversation continued, but it grew dim in Marie Léonie’s thoughts. The child and the voice disputed as to what a dollar was﻿—or so it appeared, for Marie Léonie was not familiar with either of the accents of the disputants. The child was a combative child. She drove both Gunning and the cabinetmaker Cramp with an organ of brass. Of tin perhaps, like a penny whistle. When she was not grubbily working she read books with avidity﻿—books about Blood if she could get them. She had an exaggerated respect for the Family, but none for any other soul in the world.﻿ ﻿…

Marie Léonie considered that, by now, she might have got down to the depth of the cask where you find sediment. She ran some cider into a clear glass, stopping the tube with her thumb. The cider was clear enough to let her bottle another dozen, she judged; then she would send for Gunning to take the spile-bung out of the next cask. Four sixty-gallon casks she had to attend to; two of them were done. She began to tire: she was not unfatiguable if she was indefatigable. She began at any rate to feel drowsy. She wished Valentine could have helped her. But that girl had not much backbone, and she, Marie Léonie, acknowledged that for the sake of the future it was good that she should rest and read books in Latin or Greek. And avoid nervous encounters.

She had tucked her up under an eiderdown on their four-post bed because They would have all the windows open and currents of air must, above all, be avoided by women.﻿ ﻿… *Elle* had smiled and said that it had once been her dream to read the works of Aeschylus beside the blue Mediterranean. They had kissed each other.﻿ ﻿…
===Chinese===
